— PREFACE

This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India has been prepared
for submission to the Governor under Article 151 of the Constitution of India.

This Report presents the results of the audit of the Departments of the
Government of Nagaland under Social, Economic, Revenue and General Sectors.

The cases mentioned in this Report are those which came to notice in the course of
test audit during the year 2012-13 as well as those which came to notice in earlier
years but could not be dealt with in the previous Reports. Matters relating to the
period subsequent to 2012-13 have also been included, wherever necessary.

The audits have been conducted in conformity with the Auditing Standards
issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.



VE SUMMARY

This Report contains 15 compliance audit paragraphs including Audit on
“Procurement and distribution of food stuff under Supplementary Nutrition
Programme (SNP)’ and ‘Procurement and distribution of Ration in Police
Department’ and four Performance Audits — Implementation of ‘Indira Awaas Yojana
(IAY)’, ‘Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana (RKVY)’, ‘Local Area Development
Programme (LADP)’ and ‘Roads and Bridges projects funded through Non-Lapsable
Central Pool of Resources and North Eastern Council (NLCPR/NEC)’ in Nagaland’.
According to the existing arrangements, copies of the draft audit paragraphs and draft
performance audits were sent to the Secretary of the departments concerned by the
Accountant General (Audit) with a request to furnish replies within six weeks. Replies
were not received from the Secretaries of the Departments concerned in respect of
five audit paragraphs.

Chapter-I Social Sector

A on Procurement and distribution of foodstuff under Supplement:

Nutrition Programme (SNP)

The data on the number of beneficiaries were not reliable as there was marked
difference in the report furnished to the Ministry vis-a-vis the monthly progress
reports submitted by the Child Development Project Officers. Consequently the actual
expenditure was inflated by हैं 34.53 crore during 2010-13. The Department also paid
र₹ 40.70 crore without actual receipt of material. The Department recorded excess
receipt of 22255.92 quintals of two items of foodstuff rendering the transactions
doubtful which needs further investigation. There were serious flaws in
implementation of the scheme in the State as the quantity of foodstuff required to be
provided were compromised due to short distribution of foodstuff valued at हैं 32.33
crore. The beneficiaries in the test checked projects were deprived of nutrition by
र₹ 1.48 per day during the last five years. Out of 62 AWCs in 14 test checked projects,
54 AWCs did not have kitchen sheds which defeated the objective of providing Hot
Cooked Meals.

(Paragraph 1.3)

Compliance Audit Paragraph

The Executive Engineer, PWD (Housing) Youth Resources and Sports Division,
Kohima paid an amount of % 38.31 lakh against an un-executed item of work i.e.
construction of Swimming Pool at Dimapur Club by recording false measurement.

(Paragraph 1.4)


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2012

P

rformanc its

Implementation of Indira Awaas Yojana (IAY) in Nagaland

Strategic planning process in the Department was inadequate as periodical survey to
identify the shelterless rural poor for updating permanent waitlist and preparation of
annual plan was not conducted. Selection of beneficiaries without the knowledge of
Village Development Boards was noticed. Selection of beneficiaries was not adhered
to the IAY permanent waitlist. Actual status and impact of the implementation could
not be assessed as the Department followed the waitlist prepared on the basis of BPL
census of 2002-2007.

The Department failed to provide complete habitable dwelling unit to the
beneficiaries as they were denied admissible due share under the scheme. The quality
of IAY house was compromised to project higher achievement figures. Deprivation of
beneficiaries was noticed as a result of pilferage of CGI sheets.

There was poor financial control mechanism in the Department which resulted in
deduction of Gol share, non-accountal of funds, non-submission of utilisation
certificates and avoidable expenditure on procurement of CGI sheets.

(Paragraph 2.3)

Implementation of Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana in Nagaland

The RKVY was implemented initially by seven agriculture and allied departments
and from 2012-13 two more departments (Irrigation and flood control and Co-
operation) were included. The project implementation was practically started from
2008-09 though GOI had released funds during 2007-08 to the State. The State
prepared C-SAP out of C-DAP of all the districts. There were many anomalies in C-
DAP/SAP which needs to be addressed. Delay and inadequate SLSC meetings
resulted in delay in release of funds from GOL There was also delay in release of
funds from the State to implementing Departments and nodal department which
resulted in delay in project implementation. There was no competitive bidding system
followed for procurements. Undue benefits were given to select few suppliers and
contractors. During project implementation short/non-supply of materials, wasteful
expenditure, non-achievement of target, irregularities in supply of benefits, excess
payment to suppliers and abandonment of projects were noticed. Proper mechanism
to monitor and evaluate the contribution of production at State level did not exist.
Also at the department levels, proper mechanism for post monitoring and evaluation
of the projects was absent which resulted in failure to assess the success of the
projects.

Though the State of Nagaland achieved an overall average annual growth of 5.19 per
cent (an excess of 1.19 per cent) above national target during 117 plan under
Agriculture and allied sectors. The schemes under RKVY were being implemented by
9 departments and the expenditure was thinly spread over the State. Thus, audit could

Vili


Executive Summary

not determine whether the implementation of RKVY projects had contributed to
increase of agricultural growth rate in the State.

(Paragraph 2.4)

Implementation of Local Area Development Programme in Nagaland

Village profile was not created to identify the critical areas wherein developments
were required. Decentralised policy in selection of projects was not adopted which
resulted in random selection of projects by elected members. Inadequate technical
estimates approved by DPDB resulted in short execution, diversion of projects and
overlapping of projects. Several instances of un-executed projects were noticed and
execution of non-permissible projects outside the purview of scheme guidelines had
defeated the purpose for which the scheme was designed. Verification committee
certified works without recording measurement to support the nature of assets created.
During the period 2008-13 an amount of = 270.42 crore was spent on four categorised
LADP programmes in the State. Test-check revealed 146 non-existent projects worth
% 5.18 crore indicating possible misappropriation to that extent. Neither nodal officer
nor the technical expert group at districts monitored the projects during the execution
to assess the quality of LADP funded works.

(Paragraph 2.5)
Audit on Roads & Bridges projects funded by Non-Lapsable Central Pool of|

Resources (NLCPR) and North Eastern Council (NEC) in Nagaland

Planning process adopted by the Department of Public Works was inadequate for long
term perspective plan. DPRs and technical estimates prepared were not based on
adequate survey and investigation reports resulting in deviation from the approved
DPRs and many items of works remained un-executed. Due to delay in transfer of
funds to executing divisions as well as un-authorised deduction of funds by Finance
Department, many projects remained incomplete. Undue benefit to the contractors
was provided through revisions of approved DPRs and payment for un-executed items
of work. Due to lack of adequate monitoring mechanism and sub-standard
workmanship, quality of works executed remained poor.

(Paragraph 2.6)

Com ce Audit Paragraphs

The Controller also paid an excess amount of हैं 64.12 lakh to contractors against five

Chapter-II] Economic Sector (Public Sector Undertakings)

As on 31 March 2013, the investment of State and Central Government (Capital and
long term loans) in six SPSUs was % 98.04 crore. As on 31 March 2013, of the total
investment in SPSUs, 95 per cent was in five working SPSUs and remaining 5 per
cent was in one non-working SPSU. The total investment consisted of 37 per cent
towards capital and 63 percent in long term loans.

(Paragraph 3.3)

The losses of working SPSUs during 2008-09 to 20012-13 showed a mixed trend. The
overall losses incurred by five working SPSUs increased from % 1.36 crore (2008-09)
to हैं 2.57 crore (2009-10) and again from % 2.07 crore (2010-11) to हैं 2.50 crore
(2011-12). During the five year period 2008-13, the losses were lowest in 2012-13 at
र₹ 1.01 crore.

(Paragraph 3.6)

Arrears in finalisation of Accounts

None of the working SPSUs had finalised up to date accounts and as of September
2013 total 30 accounts relating to 5 working SPSUs were still in arrears. Thus,
concrete steps should be taken by the SPSUs for preparation of accounts as per the
statutory requirements with special focus on clearance of arrears in a time bound
manner,

(Paragraph 3.7)

udit on Procurement and di ; i Department

Audit observed that the estimation and provisioning were not commensurate with the
actual feeding strength which had resulted in injudicious excess expenditure of
र₹ 29.20 crore without corresponding benefits to the personnel. There were instances
of payments without actual receipt of materials by the PCS and in the selected units.
Budgeting was unrealistic as the actual expenditure incurred exceeded the original
budget ranging from 19 per cent to 91 per cent during 2008-09 to 2012-13. The
Department not only incurred an amount of & 4.29 crore on clearance of fictitious
liability but also created an additional committed liability of € 4.45 crore for the
future.

The bidding process was not transparent and biased as the lowest bidders were not
awarded the supply works resulting in avoidable excess expenditure of हैं 1.72 crore.
The contracts were awarded based on limited tender involving very few contractors.
Hence, competitive rates were not obtained which indicate possibility of cartelization.
The Department did not devise any system for evaluation of the quality and quantity
of ration supplied to the personnel. Audit survey indicated that the personnel were not
fully satisfied with the quality of ration issued and the frequency of issue was also

12 Planning and Conduct of Audit

Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various departments of
Government based on expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of activities, level
of delegated financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls and concerns of
stake holders.

After completion of audit of each unit on a test check basis, Inspection Reports
containing audit findings are issued to the heads of the departments. The departments
are to furnish replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the
Inspection Reports. Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled
based on reply/action taken or further action is required by the auditee for compliance.
Some of the important audit observations arising out of the Inspection Reports are
processed for inclusion in the Audit reports, which are submitted to the Governor of
the State under Article 151 of the constitution of India for being laid on the table of
the Legislature.

During the year, test check of audits involving expenditure of हैं 3340.73 crore
(including funds pertaining to previous years audited during the year) of the State
Government under Social sector were conducted. This Chapter contains findings of
audit on procurement and distribution of food stuff under Supplementary Nutrition
Programme and one compliance audit paragraph.



Chapter -1 Social Sector

SOCIAL WELFARE DEPARTMENT

1.3. AUDIT ON PROCUREMENT AND DISTRIBUTION OF FOOD STUFF}

UNDER SUPPLEMENTARY NUTRITION PROGRAMME.
Highlights

The Department inflated the beneficiary list by 273384 and sent it to the Ministry and
obtained excess funds of € 34.53 crore.

(Paragraph 1.3.8.1)

The Department received 25364.30 quintals of food items valued at % 36.25 crore but
paid % 75.11 crore for supply of 48991.66 quintals which resulted in excess payment
for 23627.36 quintals amounting to हैं 38.86 crore. The possibility of misappropriation
of 38.86 crore cannot be ruled out.

(Paragraph 1.3.8.3)

Against the supply order for 484144.27 kgs of surho rice cereal valued at % 7.11 crore,
478065 kgs of weaning food of a lower cost valued at हैं 5.27 crore was supplied and
accepted by the Department which resulted in excess payment of € 1.84 crore.

(Paragraph 1.3.8.4)

The 14 test checked projects required foodstuff valued at हैं 70.28 crore. However,
these projects received foodstuff valued at % 37.95 crore resulting in short distribution
of foodstuff valued at हैं 32.33 crore.

(Paragraph 1.3.8.5)

Against the actual per capita expenditure of = 3.75 per beneficiary per day for the
State of Nagaland during the last five years, the actual per capita receipts in respect of
the 14 test checked projects was only हैं 2.27. Thus, the beneficiaries in those test
checked projects were deprived of = 1.48 per day.

(Paragraph 1.3.8.6)

Against the Union Ministry’s decision to provide 80 grams ready to cat, only 12
grams of fortified food was provided by the Department which impeded the
successful implementation of the objective of the scheme.

(Paragraph 1.3.8.7)

Physical verification of 62 AWCs in 14 selected CDPOs revealed that 54 AWCs did
not have kitchen sheds. Thus, the objective of providing Hot Cooked Meals was
defeated.

(Paragraph 1.3.8.9)

1.3.1 Introduction

The Integrated Child Development Scheme (ICDS) aims at enhancing nutritional and
health status of children below the age of 6 years, Pregnant Women, lactating mothers



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

and of late adolescent girls. Supplementary Nutrition Programme is a component of
ICDS which covers three categories of beneficiaries such as children in the age group
of 0 to 6 years, severely malnourished children and pregnant and lactating mothers.
The scheme is implemented on a matching share basis of 90:10 between the Centre
and the State Government. The revised guideline of the scheme effective from
November 2008 specifies the following financial norms.

Table No.1.3.1

1 Children 6-72 months =4

3 Pregnant women and nursing mothers w5

Source: Scheme guidelines

The scheme aimed to bridge the protein-energy gap between the Recommended
Dietary Allowance (RDA) and Average Dietary Intake (ADI). The Department
reported to have covered a total of 18,50,374 beneficiaries at a total cost of = 186.13
crore during the last five years.

1.3.2 _ Scope of Audit

The Audit was carried out by test check of records in the office of the Director, Social
Welfare Department and 14 Child Development Project Officers (CDPOs) out of 59
CDPOs involving 62 Anganwadi Centres (AWCs) out of 3455 AWCs for the period
from 2008-09 to 2012-13. The selection of CDPOs was determined through random
sampling based on the number of beneficiaries and the number of AWCs. An entry
conference with the representatives of the Social Welfare Department was held on 30
July 2013 wherein the objectives, criteria, scope and methodology of the audit was
discussed. On completion of the audit, an exit conference with the Department
represented by the Additional Secretary, Government of Nagaland, the Director and
Officers was held on 17 October 2013 to discuss the findings of audit.

1.3.3 4001 Objectives

The audit on Procurement and distribution of food stuff under Supplementary
Nutrition Programme in Nagaland was carried out to assess whether:

* requirements were assessed on the basis of actual beneficiaries
e the food stuff and nutritional supplements were supplied regularly, as per
entitlement and provided to all intended beneficiaries.

1.3.4 Audit Criteria

The main sources of audit criteria were obtained from the following:

e Scheme guidelines
e Financial Rules



Chapter -1 Social Sector

e Instructions/circulars/orders issued by the Government
e The monthly, quarterly and annual returns of the project officers.

extended by the officers of the Director Social Welfare, the Child Development
Project Officers and the Anganwadi Workers in conduct of this audit.

1.3.8.7 Shortfall in implementation of re’

According to sub-clause (a) of clause 5 of the revised guidelines for nutritional and
feeding norms issued by the Ministry of Women and Child Development, GOI, State
Government should arrange to serve more than one meal and may arrange to provide
morning snack in the form of milk/banana/egg/seasonal fruits/micronutrient fortified
food etc. since the children of the age group of 3 to 6 years are not capable of
consuming a meal of 500 calories in one sitting. The Ministry also decided (January
2006) to provide 50 per cent Recommended Dietary Allowance (RDA) through 80
grams of ready-to-eat (RTE) energy.

Audit observed that the revised feeding norms had not been adhered to by the
Department and against the Ministry’s decision of 80 grams RTE, only 12 grams° of
fortified food was provided.

As per information furnished to audit, the Department was distributing food items

uniformly to all the projects without receiving any indents from the CDPOs or
assessing the requirements on the basis of the number of beneficiaries. As discussed
in Paragraph 1.3.8.5 audit observed shortfall in receipt of food items by the test
checked projects which in turn affected even distribution to the AWCs. It was further
observed from 49 AWCs that food items were not provided to the beneficiaries at
regular intervals as per scale. Against the norm of 25 days in a month 49 AWCs out of
62 AWCs visited provided food to the beneficiaries on an average ranging from | to
20 days in a month as depicted in the pie chart.

As could be seen from the chart above, none of the AWCs provided food as per the

cent provided for 6 to 10 days and 20 per cent of the centres provided food only for a
maximum of five days. This irregular distribution of supplementary food to the
beneficiaries impeded successful implementation of the objectives of the scheme.

The Department while accepting the facts (October 2013) stated that irregular
distribution was taken seriously and also assured to ensure regular distribution of
foodstuff to the Project Officers in future. The Department termed the audit findings
as positive indicators for successful implementation of the scheme.

Kitchen, sanitation and safe d ing wate:

Clause 8 of the guidelines stipulated that Food and Nutrition Board in collaboration
with the State Government should carry out periodic checks to ensure that prescribed
standards are adhered to and quality and nutritive value of supplementary nutrition is
maintained. In case of Hot Cooked Meal it should be ensured that it is prepared in
proper kitchen sheds having adequate sanitation and safe drinking water so as to
maintain hygienic conditions.

Physical verification of 62 AWCs in 14 test checked projects revealed that 54 AWCs
did not have kitchen sheds. Thus, the objective of providing Hot Cooked Meals was
defeated.

It was also observed in audit that none of the 62 AWCs visited had drinking water
facility and sanitation as envisaged in the scheme guidelines.

Qual
As per information provided to audit, the items offered by the NGOs were selected on
the basis of Laboratory analysis report issued by the Food & Nutrition Board, Eastern
Region, Kolkata. However, laboratory analysis report did not mention the name of the
manufacturer/ batch/ date of manufacture etc. Subsequent tests were not conducted for

suc

any of those items to ensure that the approved items and quality were actually

13


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

supplied. Thus, audit could not confirm that the samples tested and the food stuff
actually supplied were the same quality and brand.

The Department in reply (October 2013) stated that the original packing were not sent
to the Laboratory for testing.

1.3.8.11 Conclusion

The data on the number of beneficiaries were not reliable as there was marked
difference in the report furnished to the Ministry vis-a-vis the monthly progress
reports submitted by the CDPOs. Consequently, the actual expenditure was inflated
by & 34.53 crore during 2010-13. The Department also paid ~ 40.70 crore without
actual receipt of materials. The Department recorded excess receipt of 22255.92
quintals of two items of foodstuff rendering the transactions doubtful which needs
further investigation. There were serious flaws in implementation of the scheme in the
State as the quantity of foodstuff required to be provided were compromised due to
short distribution of foodstuff valued at % 32.33 crore. The beneficiaries in the test
checked projects were deprived of nutrition by हैं 1.48 per day during the last five
years. Out of 62 AWCs in 14 test checked projects, 54 AWCs did not have kitchen
sheds which defeated the objective of providing Hot Cooked Meals.

1.3.8.12 Recommendations

> The data on the number of beneficiaries needs to be updated based on the
reports furnished by the Anganwadi Workers and the reports furnished to the
Ministry should also be strictly monitored.

> Proper monitoring and accounting mechanisms should be established to
ensure that payments to the suppliers are released only after receipt of
material.

> The required quantity and quality of foodstuff prescribed in the guidelines of
the scheme should be regularly provided.

> Distribution of foodstuff to the AWCs should be closely monitored to ensure
that beneficiaries are regularly provided the scale of nutrition.

ious Expenditure

The Executive Engineer, PWD (Housing) Youth Resources and Sports Division,

Kohima paid an amount of हैं 38.31 lakh against an unexecuted item of work i.e.
construction of Swimming Pool at Dimapur Club by recording false measurement.

Rule 354 of the Nagaland Public Works Department Code provides that the actual
measurements form the basis for practically every payment made in the Public Works
Department and therefore, their importance has been emphasised from time to time. It
further provides that it is not permissible for Disbursing Officers to relax the rules
regarding the check-measurements. The detailed instructions in this regard have also
been laid down for the purpose.

14


Chapter -1 Social Sector

The Youth Resources and Sports (YRS) Department sanctioned (February 2012) an
amount of & 50 lakh’ for ‘Construction of Swimming Pool at Dimapur Club’ on the
basis of an estimate prepared by the Executive Engineer (EE), PWD (Housing), YRS
Division® which was duly approved by the Chief Engineer, PWD (Housing). The
work was awarded (March 2012) to the lowest bidder’ at हैं 38.31 lakh as per NPWD
Schedule of Rates 2010 with a stipulation to complete the work within 12 months.
Director, YRS drew हैं 42.14 lakh (March 2012) and remitted to the EE (May/July
2012)

Scrutiny of Measurement Book (MB) (March 2013) revealed that the contractor
commenced the work on 9 April 2012 and completed the work on 10 August 2012
which was measured on three different dates from April-August 2012 by the JE, SDO
and EE of the Engineering Division. The contractor was paid = 38.31!° lakh (August
2012)! as per work order. The remaining amount of & 3.83 lakh meant for Site had
not been utilised (March 2013) and was kept in the bank account of the division.

As the work was reportedly completed within a short period of five months against
the stipulated period of 12 months, audit conducted a physical verification to ascertain
the actual status of work. During joint physical verification conducted (March 2013)
by audit alongwith the EE, YRS Division, it was seen that Swimming pool was not
constructed at Dimapur Club contrary to the entries made in the MB and progress
report submitted by the EE. The EE however, stated that a building committee was
formed for this project by the members of Dimapur Club and the committee decided
to go for a pre-fabricated swimming pool as the amount granted was less. The
committee had ordered the pre-fabricated swimming pool which had not arrived.
However, the EE could not offer any explanation for falsely recording the
measurement and releasing the payment.

Thus, the EE made a fictitious payment of € 38.31 lakh against unexecuted items of
work by recording false measurement of work.

In reply (June 2013), the Government stated that the work was held up because of the
change of location and scope of work due to unstable soil conditions in the original
site. The Government further stated that the fund was released to the contractor as
requested by him for purchase of equipment for the project and the civil works were
in good progress.

2.2 Planning and Conduct of Audit

194.88

Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various departments of
Government based on expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of activities, level
of delegated financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls and concerns of
stake holders.

After completion of audit of each unit on a test check basis, Inspection Reports
containing audit findings are issued to the heads of the departments. The departments
are to furnish replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the
Inspection Reports. Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled
based on reply/action taken or further action is required by the auditee for compliance.
Some of the important audit observations arising out of the Inspection Reports are
processed for inclusion in the Audit reports, which are submitted to the Governor of
the State under Article 151 of the constitution of India for laying on the table of the
Legislature.

During the year, test check of audits involving expenditure of € 1542.95 crore
(including funds pertaining to previous years audited during the year) of the State
Government under Economic Sector were conducted. This chapter contains four
Performance Audits and five compliance audit paragraphs.

18


Chapter-II Economic Sector

RURAL DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT

2.3 Implementation of Indira A Nagaland

During 2002 an integrated performance audit of the Rural Housing Scheme of the
Rural Development Department, Government of Nagaland (GON) was taken up
which was incorporated in the Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG)
for the year ended 31 March 2002, inter-alia covering Indira Awaas Yojana (IAY).
The audit findings were discussed by the Public Accounts Committee (PAC) of the
State Legislature in September 2011 and May 2012. To assess the impact of
implementation of IAY and to evaluate the action taken by the Department on the
recommendation, performance audit of IAY scheme was taken up during April to

August 2013. The performance audit revealed that the objectives of the Scheme could
not be entirely achieved due to lack of proper planning, poor monitoring, vulnerability
to frauds etc., despite being pointed out in earlier audit. The major observations
noticed are highlighted below:

Highlights

There was no documentary evidence in support of distribution of 28038 bundles of
CGI sheets valued at = 12.77 crore.

(Paragraph 2.3.9.1(B))

In two selected villages of Mon district IAY was not implemented as CGI sheets
worth हैं 30.51 lakh did not reach the beneficiaries. Short distribution of 1198 bundles
of CGI sheets costing = 55.53 lakh was reported during interview of 935 beneficiaries
of four selected districts.

(Paragraph 2.3.10.4 and 2.3.10.5)

Annual Accounts of 11 DRDAs exhibited inflated expenditure figure of € 2.37 crore
on procurement of CGI sheets resulting in possible misappropriation of scheme funds.

(Paragraph 2.3.11.6)

The Department did not take any initiative to correspond with other line departments
for convergence of IAY programme. Poor monitoring and internal control mechanism
had adversely affected the overall implementation of the scheme.

(Paragraphs 2.3.12 and 2.3.13)

2.3.1 Introduction

Indira Awaas Yojana (IAY) was launched in the State as a Centrally Sponsored
Scheme in 1985-86 with the objective of providing assistance for construction of
houses to shelterless rural people living below the poverty line (BPL) and belonging
primarily to the Scheduled Castes (SCs), Scheduled Tribes (STs) and freed bonded
labour, widows and next of-kin to defence personnel killed in action. Implementation
of IAY in Nagaland was restricted to providing housing materials in the form of CGI

19


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

sheets during the period 2008-13. During the last five years period (2008-09 to
2012-13) the Government of Nagaland received % 217.17 crore against which an
expenditure of हैं 214.21 crore had been incurred leaving an unspent balance of
र₹ 2.96 crore.

ional set up

The organisational setup for implementation of IAY in the State is given below:

2.. Scope of Audit

The implementation of IAY in the State of Nagaland for the period from 2008-09 to
2012-13 was reviewed as a follow up to the CAG Report on Rural Housing (Indira
Awaas Yojana) for the year ended March 2002 through a test check of records in the
Directorate of Rural Development Department, Project Directors (District Rural
Development Agency), Block Development Officers and Village Development
Boards concerned and interview of selected beneficiaries at the village level.

Implementation of Indira Awaas Yojana in the State during 2008-09 to 2012-13 was
reviewed in audit during April to August 2013 by a test check of records of
Directorate of RD Department, 4 DRDAs’ out of 11 and 8? BDOs out of 52 in the
State. An expenditure of = 105.41 crore representing 49 per cent of the total
expenditure (¥ 214.21 crore) was test checked.

The present performance audit covered four districts, four DRDAs, eight BDOs and
80 VDBs out of 1129 in the State by adopting sampling methods of “Probability
Proportional to Size With Replacement (PPSWR) and Simple Random Sampling
Without Replacement (SRSWOR).

Chapter-II Economic Sector

2.3.4 Audit objectives
The objectives of the performance audit were to assess whether:

(i) planning, identification and selection of beneficiaries conform to the scheme
provisions;

(ii) targets and achievements and technology of constructions correspond to the
quality and financial parameters ;

(iii) the allocation and release of funds under IAY were made in an adequate and
timely manner and utilised economically and efficiently;

(iv) convergence of the IAY activities with other programmes and non-
governmental stakeholders as envisaged was effectively achieved and ensured
availability of a complete functional dwelling unit; and

(v) the mechanism in place for monitoring and evaluation of the outcomes of the
programme were adequate and effective.

2.3.5 Audit criteria

The performance was assessed with reference to the following sources of criteria:

> Guidelines of [AY issued by the Ministry of Rural Development ;

> Outcome budget of the Ministry of Rural Development;

> Periodical reports/returns prescribed by State Government;

> Circulars/instructions issued by the Department of Rural Development, Gol
and State;

Reports of National Level Monitors with the Ministry;

Studies conducted by the Planning Commission and various agencies at
Central level.

2.3.6 Audit methodology

The performance audit commenced with an entry conference (April 2013) with the
Commissioner & Secretary, RDD and other departmental officers to discuss the audit
objectives. The audit findings were discussed in an exit conference held on 28
October 2013. The replies furnished by the Government have been appropriately
incorporated in the report.

Vv

audit findings

A review of implementation of IAY scheme for the period 1997-98 to 2001-02
revealed that the Department embarked upon the scheme without collecting the
information about the shortage of houses in the State. The scheme was launched with
effect from 1985-86 in the State but a survey of the population living below the
poverty line (BPL) was conducted only during 1997-98. The coverage of non-BPL
group still continues. The Department provides only 5 bundles of corrugated
galvanised iron sheet (CGI), roofing material to each beneficiary without considering
other required elements for construction of house as per guidelines which amounts to
denial of full benefit of the housing scheme to the poor people. The implementation of

21


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

the scheme suffered due to procurement of roofing materials at high cost from local
suppliers instead of from manufacturers. The performance reported by the Department
was not based on facts. The achievement figures were inflated.

The audit findings were discussed by the Public Accounts Committee vide its 100"
Report adopted on 6" September 2011 and presented to the House on 150 September
2011.

2.3.8 Acknowledgement

The office of the Accountant General (Audit), Nagaland places on record our sincere
appreciation for the co-operation of the Rural Development Department, the Store
Keeper Central Godown, Officers and staff of District Rural Development Agencies,
Block Development Officers and Secretaries of Village Development Boards.

Audit findings

Audit objective 1: To ascertain whether planning, identification and selection of|
beneficiaries conform to the scheme provisions

2.3.9.1 Identification and Selection of Beneficiaries

According to para 2.1 of IAY guidelines, District Rural Development Agencies
(DRDAs) shall decide the number of houses to be constructed during a particular
financial year. The beneficiaries will be selected by the Village Development Boards
(VDBs) from the Permanent IAY Waitlists prepared on the basis of BPL lists in order
of seniority in the list. The Gram Panchayats may draw out the shelterless families
from the BPL list strictly in the order of ranking in the list.

(A) Assessment of housing shortage was not done in the State by any of the test
checked DRDAs. The DRDAs prepared a Permanent Waitlist during 2007 basing on
the BPL Census 2002-2007. Regular updation of the Permanent Waitlist was also not
done in the test checked districts.

During scrutiny of records of four test checked districts the following irregularities
were noticed:

(9 Selection of beneficiaries outside permanent waitlist:

8259 bundles of CGI sheets worth हैं 3.78 crore were distributed to 1,378 beneficiaries
of 76 VDBs covering 8 blocks of 3 districts outside the list of BPL. Names of those
beneficiaries were also not traceable in the IAY permanent waitlist. The details are
shown in Appendix-2.3.1).

(ii) Selection of beneficiaries from APL category:

In 16 VDBs of 3 Blocks at 2 districts 80 Above Poverty Line category (APL) persons
were given the benefit of IAY scheme in contravention of IAY guidelines. As a result,
524 bundles of CGI sheets worth % 0.25 crore were distributed to non-entitled
beneficiaries. The details are shown in Appendix-2.3.2).

22


Chapter-II Economic Sector

(iii) Multiple allotment to the same beneficiaries

3760 bundles of CGI sheets worth हैं 1.76 crore were distributed to 693 beneficiaries
on multiple occasions in 255 Village Development Boards covering 7 blocks of 3
districts as detailed in Appendix-2.3.3. This was despite the fact that each beneficiary
was entitled to receive the benefits only once.

The above irregularities are indicators of the fact that the permanent waitlist was not
strictly adhered to while selecting beneficiaries as envisaged in the Scheme
guidelines. Allotment of IAY houses to beneficiaries who were not listed in the
permanent wait list, APL category households and multiple allotments to same
beneficiaries resulted in denial of IAY benefits to other eligible persons who were
listed in the permanent wait list.

(B) During scrutiny of records it was noticed that six District Rural Development
Agencies made allocation and issued 28,038 bundles of CGI sheets stating it to be
Assembly Constituency/District Rural Development Agency/Block allocations. VDBs
did not have any records of receipt and distribution of the CGI sheets as they were
neither intimated nor involved in selection of those beneficiaries.

There was no visible evidence and documents in support of actual distribution
of 28,038 bundles of CGI sheets valued at % 12.77 crore at the village level
(Appendix-2.3.4).

Thus, selection of beneficiaries in contravention to the guidelines had frustrated the
objective of the scheme to that extent.

The Department stated (November 2013) that the villagers approached their elected
representatives for assistance under the scheme and their applications were forwarded
to the DRDAs/Blocks. The recommendations were screened out and eligible
applicants were assisted. However, the recommendations of elected members were
taken into account strictly on the basis of the beneficiary being BPL.

The reply was not tenable as the authority for selection of beneficiaries lies solely on
the Village Development Boards and permanent waitlist was prepared at the VDB
level in consultation with the villagers/their representatives. Hence, approaching of
the elected representatives by the villagers for selection of their names as beneficiaries
of IAY scheme without involving VDBs was beyond the scheme guidelines.

As per para 2.2 of IAY guidelines prioritisation of beneficiaries will be given to
(i) Freed bonded labourers, (ii) SC/ST households, (iii) Families/widows of personnel
from defence services/paramilitary forces, killed in action, (iv) Non- SC/ST BPL
households and (v) Physically and mentally challenged persons. The selection of the
beneficiaries will be subject to the condition that the households of all the above
categories except (iii) are Below Poverty Line (BPL).

23


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

Out of 68,805 IAY dwelling units constructed in the State during the period 2008-13,
65948 dwelling units were allotted to male, 806 allotted to female beneficiaries and
2051 to physically or mentally challenged persons, as shown in the following table:

The Department furnished (November 2013) a list claiming coverage of 2,051
beneficiaries under the category of physically or mentally challenged persons. The list
furnished by the Department was not supported with documentary evidence to
substantiate that the beneficiaries were physically challenged persons.

Further, the Department had also failed to consider categories of beneficiaries under
families of military/paramilitary forces who died in action. The Department accepted
the fact that the beneficiaries under this category were not identified while fixing the
target of IAY housing.

Thus, the Department did not adhere to the provision of guidelines which envisages
priority in selection of beneficiaries.

Para 5.7 of the guidelines envisage that officers dealing with the IAY in the State,
District and Block levels must be trained in various disaster resistant features to be
adopted in the houses and they should ensure that this is complied with during their
field visits. In addition, local carpenters and masons should be trained for skill
upgradation and use of low cost technology and local material under the SGSY. The
awareness among the beneficiaries must be created about the disaster resistant and
environment friendly technology through exhibitions of low cost technologies at the
district and block level, seminars, workshops etc. The services of State Institutes of
Rural Developments (SIRDs), Extension Training Centres may be obtained for this
purpose.

The Department stated (September 2013) that during the period 2008-09 to 2012-13
no formal training had been conducted. The Department further stated that matters
relating to implementation of IAY were briefed to the VDB secretaries during Annual
Conference held at the district level. The reply of the Department is not tenable as the
training should have been imparted to the beneficiaries, carpenters and masons. The
Department also could not furnish any documentary evidence with regard to the
discussion of IAY matters during such conference.

Chapter-II Economic Sector

Audit objective 2: To ascertain whether targets and achievements and|
technology of constructions (constructions of new houses, renovation, providing
land etc.) correspond to the quality and financial parameters

2.3.10.1__Type design, amenities and cost effectiveness

The State Rural Department approved type design of [AY houses’ with a plinth area
of 50.69 sqm. The approved type design includes the additional amenities of a
kitchen, smokeless chulla, sanitary facilities (toilets). However, this type of design
was not followed. The Department also did not insist the beneficiaries to follow this
type design. During interview the beneficiaries expressed their lack of awareness of
the JAY house design.

The unit cost for an IAY house was हैं 48,500. The Department, however, did not fully
telease this amount to the beneficiaries. Instead, 7 bundles of CGI sheets costing
% 36,000 only was provided to the beneficiaries which was not sufficient for
construction of a dwelling unit with additional amenities. Thus, beneficiaries were
denied of basic amenities such as sanitary facilities, kitchen, smokeless chullah and
drinking water supply.

After providing 7 bundles of CGI sheets all responsibilities of construction activities
were left in the hands of the beneficiaries. The beneficiaries had no other option but to
construct the house with the locally available raw materials without any technical
assistance or supervision from the Department.

Despite being pointed out in the Audit Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General
of India for the year ended March 2002, Government of Nagaland (para 3.3.16), the
Department still continued with the system of procurement and distribution of CGI
sheets to the beneficiaries.

The year-wise targets of beneficiaries and achievements during the period 2008-09 to
2012-13 are shown in the following table:

Table No. 2.3.2

There are 18 VDBs in Tizit Block (Mon district) out of which 10 VDBs were selected

(Appendix-2.3.5) were issued to two VDBs (Neitong and Ngangting) during the
period 2008-09 to 2012-13.

However, during field visit the VDB Secretaries stated that they had not received any
CGI sheets under JAY during the aforementioned period. Certificates to this effect
had been furnished by the concerned Secretaries.

This had resulted in non-implementation of IAY scheme in the two villages and the
possibility of the materials worth हैं 30.51 lakh being misappropriated cannot be ruled
out. The matter therefore, requires further investigation.

11.7 Differential Rate of Interest Scheme

According to para 3.1 and 3.3 of the guidelines, in addition to the assistance provided
under the scheme, an IAY beneficiary can avail a loan up to हैं 20,000/- per housing
unit under Differential Rate of Interest (DRI) scheme at an interest rate of 4 per cent
per annum. It will be the responsibility of the State Governments/DRDAs concerned
to coordinate with the financial institutions to make available the credit facility to
those interested beneficiaries.

In Nagaland, no beneficiary had availed the benefit of DRI scheme during the period
2008-13. During interview the beneficiaries stated that they were not aware of the
existence of such facility.

The Department stated (November 2013) that DRI was not implementable in the State
due to non-availability of banking facilities.

2.3.12 Convergence with other schemes

Audit objective 4: To ascertain whether convergence of the IAY activities with

other programmes and non-governmental stakeholders was effectively achieved
and ensured availability of a complete functional dwelling unit.

According to provisions laid down in para 5.11 of the guidelines- the District Rural
Development Agencies (DRDAs) will make concerted efforts to identify the
programmes/schemes being implemented by various Ministries/ Departments of the
Government of India which could be dovetailed with Indira Awaas Yojana so as to
ensure that IAY beneficiaries also avail the benefits of those schemes intended for
rural BPL households.

The following programmes/schemes implemented by various Ministries/Departments
of Government of India are operational in Nagaland:

(i) Total sanitation campaign (TSC) and National Rural Water Supply
Programme (NRWSP):- Public Health Engineering Department

(ii) Rajiv Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana (RGGVY) to provide free
electricity connection to BPL families:- Power Department
(iii) Loan under Differential Rate of Interest (DRI) Scheme to enable the

beneficiaries to complete construction of the house:- _ Nationalised
Banks in Nagaland

However, the Department of Rural Development did not take any initiative to
correspond with other line Departments for convergence of IAY programme. During
interview, the beneficiaries also stated that they were unaware of such convergence
facilities.

33


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

The Department stated (November 2013) that the matter regarding convergence of
IAY scheme with other schemes was being taken up.

2.3 13 Monitoring and evaluation

Audit objective 5: To ascertain whether the mechanism in place for monitoring}
and evaluation of the outcomes of the programme were adequate and effective.

2.3.13.1__ Monitoring and evaluation framework

Officers dealing with the IAY at the State headquarters should visit districts regularly
and ascertain through field visits whether the programme was being implemented
satisfactorily and whether construction of houses was in accordance with the
prescribed procedure. Likewise, officers at the district and block levels must closely
monitor all aspects of the IAY through visits to work sites. A schedule of inspection
which prescribes a minimum number of field visits for each supervisory level
functionary from the State level to the block level should be drawn up and strictly
adhered to. The State Government should prescribe the periodical reports/returns
through which it should monitor the performance of LAY in the districts and also get
appropriate reports and returns prescribed, to be collected by the DRDAs. The
monitoring of the programme at the State Level would be the responsibility of State
Level Vigilance and Monitoring Committee for Rural Development Programmes. A
representative or nominee of the Ministry of Rural Development, Government of
India should invariably be invited to participate in the meetings of the Committee.
The reporting of complete and comprehensive data at all levels of implementation of
the scheme should be uploaded through Indira Awaas Yojana-Management
Information System (IAY-MIS) only. A web-based MIS Programme Software
‘AWAASSoft’ to capture beneficiary-wise data to monitor the LAY Scheme was also
launched on 16.7.2010.

To deal with complaints, an effective Complaint Monitoring System with adequate
staff should be set up at the State level which can independently visit the regular
execution wing and give a report to the implementing agencies about the
shortcomings/shortfall for effective redressal.

The States/UTs may conduct Evaluation Studies on their own regarding the
implementation and impact of the programme in their State.

.13.2 State Level Monitoring and Evaluation

The monitoring of the IAY scheme at the State Level will be the responsibility of
State Level Vigilance and Monitoring Committee (SLV&MC) for Rural Development
Programmes. A representative or nominee of the Ministry of Rural Development,
Government of India should invariably be invited to participate in the meetings of the
Committee. Accordingly, SLV&MC held its meeting once in a year. However, during
the last five years (2008-09 to 2012-13) no representative of the Ministry of Rural
Development, Government of India had attended the meeting.

34


Chapter-II Economic Sector

No SLV&MC meeting minutes except for the year 2009-10 could be made available
to audit. Hence, details of points discussed in the meeting and action taken thereof for
2008-09 and 2010 -13 could not be ascertained by Audit.

The Department accepted (November 2013) the fact.

2.3.13.3 Internal Verification of Works at Field Level

The Department stated (July 2013) that field inspections to oversee the
implementation of the IAY scheme were being carried out by the departmental
officers at various levels from time to time. However, scheduled programmes of the
field visits and inspection reports etc. were not maintained. In the absence of those
vital supporting documents the genuineness of field visits or inspections could not be
vouchsafed by audit. During interview, the selected beneficiaries confirmed the fact
of non-inspection by departmental officers at any stage of the [AY implementation.

The Department in reply stated (November 2013) that special conference of Project
Directors and Block Development Officers was convened in November 2013 which
would be followed by official directive.

2.3.13.4 Deployment of National Level Monitors

National Level Monitors (NLM) were deployed to inspect the implementation of rural
development programmes in the State once in a year. However copies of the
inspection reports were not available with the Department. In the absence of
inspection reports actual findings of the monitors and follow up actions taken there
against by the Department could not be checked by Audit.

Department forwarded (November 2013) a copy of NLM Report for the year 2009-10
in respect of Phek district which was not selected for detailed check.

2.3.13.5 District Level Vigilance and Monitoring Committee

The District Level Vigilance and Monitoring Committee (DLV&MC) is responsible
for monitoring the implementation of various rural development programmes at the
district level. Necessary corrective measures, if any, could be taken up and any
discrepancies at any level shall be noted down for further discussion and to take
follow up action with the concerned ministry.

The minutes provided to audit revealed that status of funds and target achievements of
various programmes were only reported in the meeting without taking up the main
issues of vigilance and monitoring for effective implementation of the programmes.
Hence, the functioning of the Vigilance and Monitoring Committee failed to
contribute positive impact for improvement in the implementation of the IAY scheme.

The Department forwarded (November 2013) the copy of minutes of DLV&MC
which had already been furnished to audit during July 2013 without any comments.

35


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

2.3.13.6 Transparency and Accountal

According to para 6.3.1 of the guidelines all vital information should invariably be
made available to people to bring about greater transparency at village, block and
district levels.

In the course of performance audit, the following cases of non-observance of
transparency and accountability at all levels were noticed.

VILLAGE LEVEL:

(i) Selection of beneficiaries was based on the BPL list prepared during
2002-07. Periodical survey for updation was not done. Permanent [AY
Waitlists was not displayed for public discourse.

(ii) Type design set for an IAY house was not made available to the
beneficiaries. Inclusion of desired features was not insisted.

(iv) Lists of selected beneficiaries were not displayed at public places.

(v) Provisions of the guidelines were not made available to the villagers.

(vi) | Permanent Indira Awaas Yojana signboards/logos were not displayed on
the allotted houses.

(vii) Beneficiaries under VIP allocations were not in the knowledge of VDBs.

BLOCK LEVEL:

6) Inventory of IAY houses constructed giving details of the date of start and
the date of completion of construction of dwelling unit, name of the village
and block in which the house is located; occupation and category of
beneficiaries and other relevant particulars was not maintained.

(ii) Details of village-wise distribution of CGI sheets along with list of
beneficiaries were not displayed for public to see.

DISTRICT LEVEL:
(i) Position of funds received and allocation, physical and financial progress

etc., were not placed in the public domain stating that the MIS for IAY
was not functional.

The Department stated (November 2013) that IAY logo was compulsorily
displayed on all houses constructed under IAY. But the fact remains that the same
was not found in the houses physically verified.

Selection of beneficiaries and implementation of the programme had been
decentralised to the VDBs. Hence, inventory was maintained at the village level.

The reply was not tenable as the audit team found that the selected VDBs
maintained only the records of receipt and distribution of CGI sheets.

36


Chapter-II Economic Sector

2.3.13.7 Social Audit

The system of social audit as envisaged in para 6.3.5 of the guidelines was not
conducted. The Department stated that there was no necessity for conducting social
audit since no monetary transactions were involved in the implementation of IAY
scheme at the village level.

However, the concept of social audit covers not only monetary transaction but also all

activities involved in the process of planning, selection of beneficiaries, distribution

of roofing materials, actual construction of the houses etc. Hence, the Department
failed to maintain transparency and accountability due to non conduct of social audit.

2.3.13.8 Impact Assessment

Other than annual visit by the National Level Monitors (NLM) no other
body/organisation at any level had ever conducted survey to assess the impact of IAY
implementation in the State. The NLM annual visit reports were also not available
with the Department.

The Department did not conduct periodical survey to identify the shelterless rural
poor for updating permanent waitlist and preparation of Annual Plan. In the absence
of evaluation reports, regularly updated Waitlists and annual plans, actual status and
impacts of the implementation could not be assessed.

The Department assured (November 2013) that the audit observations would be
complied henceforth.

2.3.13.9 Information, Education and Communication (IEC) activities

Information, Education and Communication activities in the State had not been taken

up till the date of the performance audit. The Government of Nagaland stated that
AWAASSOFT could not be made operational in the State as only 23 out of 52 Blocks
(45 per cent) had banking facilities.

On audit query, the Department stated (September 2013) that correspondence with
Gol, to relax the process of reporting the information under Fund Transfer Format of
the software and allowing submission of the reports in hard copy till such time proper
alternative mechanism is worked out or to review the IAY-MIS software and suitably
modify it to enable uploading data as per the prevailing conditions in Nagaland, had
been made.

The reply of the Department was not satisfactory as the Department has not taken any
initiative to act upon the Gol instructions of February/March 2013 to convene a
meeting with representatives of banking institutions and post office to extend banking
facilities at the village level. Moreover, only CGI sheet roofing material was provided
to the beneficiaries in lieu of cash grant. As such opening of bank account by the
beneficiaries was not necessary to avail the benefit of IAY scheme. To make the JAY-
MIS software operational, the Department needs to adopt cash payment through bank
as envisaged in the IAY guidelines instead of distributing CGI sheets.

37


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

2

4 Con

n

Strategic planning process in the Department was inadequate as periodical survey to
identify the shelterless rural poor for updating permanent waitlist and preparation of
annual plan was not conducted. Selection of beneficiaries without the knowledge of
VDBs was noticed. Selection of beneficiaries was not adhered to the 10 ४ permanent
waitlist. Actual status and impacts of the implementation could not be assessed as the
Department followed the waitlist prepared on the basis of BPL census of 2002-2007.

The Department failed to provide complete habitable dwelling unit to the
beneficiaries as they were denied admissible due share under the scheme. The quality
of IAY house was compromised to project higher achievement figures. Deprivation of
beneficiaries was noticed as a result of pilferage of CGI sheets.

There was poor financial control mechanism in the Department which resulted in
deduction of Gol share, non-accountal of funds, non-submission of utilisation
certificates and avoidable expenditure on procurement of CGI sheets.

Beneficiaries were denied the basic amenities such as, sanitary facilities, kitchen,
smokeless chullah, drinking water supply etc., as the Department failed to converge
TAY with other schemes.

The Department neither conducted field supervision to assess the progress of
construction of houses nor extended any technical assistance. Completion certificates
from the VDBs were not insisted. Complete inventory of houses were also not
maintained. As such, houses shown to audit team during inspection could not be
considered as genuine IAY houses.

Besides, the Department also failed to take remedial actions on the points raised in the
earlier audit report.

In view of the above, the achievement projected by the State Government was not
reliable and therefore, the implementation of the IAY scheme should be strengthened.

2. Recommendations

“ Planning needs to be streamlined with regular updating of permanent waitlist
prepared on the basis of BPL list in their order of seniority and preparation of
annual plans after conducting proper survey to cover all deserving
beneficiaries;

“ Regular training and awareness programmes should be conducted;
Financial control mechanism in the Department needs to be strengthened;

“+ A systematic mechanism on material management should be evolved to check
procurement of inferior quality and receipt of reduced quantity of CGI sheets;

* Maintenance of basic records needs to be ensured at all levels;

“ The system of providing only CGI sheets to the beneficiaries needs to be
reviewed;

“ Monitoring and evaluation system needs to be improved.

2.4.1 Introduction

The Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana (RKVY), also known as National Agriculture
Development Programme (NADP) is a Special Additional Central Assistance Scheme
(100 per cent grant) to State plan, launched during 2007-08 targeted to achieve the
growth rate of 4 per cent per annum in the agriculture and allied sectors during XI
Plan (2007-12) and 2012-13.

At present, the RKVY scheme is implemented by nine’? departments including
Agriculture in all the eleven!’ districts of the State.

During the period from 2007-08 to 2012-13, 116 projects and six sub-schemes under
18” Sectors were implemented involving a total cost of = 16706 lakh including
District Agriculture Plan (DAP) funds of 110 lakh.

' Agriculture, Horticulture, Veterinary and Animal Husbandry, Fisheries, Soil and Water
Conservation, Land Resources, Sericulture, Irrigation and flood control and Co-operation.

Kohima, Dimapur, Zunheboto, Phek, Kiphire, Mon, Longleng, Wokha, Peren, Tuensang and
Mokokchung.

Agriculture Mechanisation, Seed, Integrated Pest Management, Fertilizers and INM, Marketing and
Post Harvest Management, Non-farm Activities, Organic
Development, Extension, Horticulture, Animal Husbandry, Fisheries, Micro/Minor Irrigation,
Sericulture, Natural Resource Management, Innovative Programmes/‘Training/Capacity
Building/others, Research(Agri/Horti/Animal Husbandry etc) and Co-operative and Co-operation.

‘arming/Bio fertilizers, Crops

Chapter-II Economic Sector

=

At Government of India (GOI) level, Department of Agriculture (DAC) and at the
State level, the State Agriculture Department is the nodal Department for
implementation of the scheme. At State level SLSC was constituted in February 2008
with Chief Secretary as the Chairman for sanctioning of the projects and to review the
implementation of the scheme and its objectives so as to ensure that the projects are
implemented in accordance with the guidelines laid down by the GOI. Agriculture
Production Management Committee headed by Principal Secretary, Agriculture as
Vice Chairman.

At the district level, “District Level Committee (DLC) on RKVY/NADP” comprising
District Officers of Agriculture and allied departments under the Chairmanship of
Deputy Commissioner (DC) was constituted in April 2008. DLC is responsible for

41


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

implementation of the scheme in coordination with the Village Committees. The
District Agriculture officer (Horticulture Officer in certain districts) is the nodal
officer and is responsible for coordinating with the allied departments in scheme
implementation.

2.4.2 pe of Audit

The instant Audit covered the period from 2007-2013 to ascertain the economy and
efficiency in implementation by the departments, transparency and effectiveness of
various programmes under RKVY through a test check of records of the Directorates
of three departments namely Agriculture, Horticulture and Veterinary and Animal
Husbandry along with their subordinate offices in the five selected/sampled districts
during May to August 2013 including visits to 37 villages.

Out of 18 sectors and six sub-schemes implemented, six sectors’? and two sub-
schemes were covered for performance audit and three projects were selected from
each sector using Probability Proportional to Size Without Replacement (PPSWOR)
method for test check. Simple Random Sampling Without Replacement (SRSWOR)
method was adopted for selection of five'* out of 11 districts. In each district,
minimum of two to seven villages having implemented more than two or more
common selected sectors was picked for test check and physical verification.

The main audit objectives were to assess whether:

> Planning process of the implementation of the scheme was effective and
according to the guidelines;

> Financial management ensured adequate and timely availability of funds and
their effective and economic utilisation;

> Projects were implemented in economical, efficient and effective manner.

> Internal control mechanisms were put in place to ensure efficient and effective
monitoring and control over implementation; monitoring mechanism at each
level was adequate.

> The objectives of maximising returns to the farmers in Agriculture and allied
sectors were achieved and the State could achieve four per cent growth in the
agricultural sector during the XIth Plan period.

The audit findings were benchmarked against the criteria obtained from the following
sources:

> Guidelines stating the implementation principles of RKVY in the department

of Agriculture and allied departments.

Audit coverage and Methodology

Audit plan included the audit objectives, criteria and the plan for field work which
was shared with the Government through entry conference held in April 2013 and the
audit was conducted from May to August 2013 which included detailed review, issue
of questionnaire, gathering of documentary and photographic evidence etc. The
findings were discussed in an exit conference held in November 2013 and replies of
the Department of Agriculture and Horticulture have been incorporated appropriately
in the Report.

Audit findings

2.4.6 Audit Objective: Planning process of implementation of the Scheme was
effective and according to RKVY guidelines

2.4.6.1 Preparation and submission of DAP (District Agriculture Plan)/SAP
(State Agriculture Plan)

District Agriculture Plan (DAP) is an initiative taken in response to main theme of the
11" Five Year Plan. One of the main aims of RKVY is preparation of the agricultural
development plan from Gram Panchayat upwards to district level. This is different
from the earlier agriculture planning exercise which broadly followed the top-down
approach and began with the State level fixing of the targets which were
disaggregated to the districts and down below:

The DAP as envisaged in paragraph 3.1 of the Guideline for implementation of
RKVY/NADP (August, 2007) was intended to present the vision for Agriculture and
allied sectors within the overall development perspective of the district and would
present the financial requirement and the sources of financing the agriculture
development plans in a comprehensive way by including the resources available from
other existing District, State or Central schemes, etc. and adhere to the guidelines
circulated by the Planning Commission for District Planning.

As per paragraph 3.2 of C-DAP Manual of Planning Commission (March, 2008),
Agriculture Planning Unit at different levels needs to be constituted involving a group
of experts from various disciplines relevant to the needs of the area. Accordingly,
District Agriculture Planning Unit (DAPU), Block Agriculture Planning Unit (BAPU)
and Village Agriculture Planning Unit (VAPU) was to be established for effective

43


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

planning. Where Agriculture Technology Management Agency (ATMA) was
established, it should support and supplement all the functions of DAPU/BAPU.

Para 3.3 of C-DAP Manual envisages that DAPU would be responsible for
preparation of C-DAP with the help of Technical Support Institution (TSI) and Plan-
plus software package from NIC.

C-DAP involve preparation of the agricultural development plan from Gram
Panchayat/Village level upwards to district level. The Village level planning is the
most important exercise in the preparation of C-DAP. The essential focus of this
exercise is to empower grass-root institutions in the planning process and
understanding the development initiatives at the grass-root. Participatory preparation
of the document can ensure its ownership by all stakeholders, who subsequently may
share responsibility to work for making it happen. The C-DAPs so prepared must be
approved by the concerned District Planning Committee (DPC).

It was noticed in audit that the C- DAP were prepared for all the districts. The State at
the district level had constituted “District Level Committee of NADP/RKVY”
comprising Agriculture and allied departments. The Agriculture Planning Unit at the
Village and Block level had not been constituted. The C- DAP was prepared by
District Agriculture and allied Committee based on the proposals submitted by the
Agriculture and allied departments. The C- DAP as approved by the Deputy
Commissioner, Chairman District Level Committee of NADP/RKVY was forwarded
to the Nodal Agriculture Department.

Further, the districts did not prepare a shelf of projects under Stream-I for posing to
the SLSC. Instead all project proposals were prepared from Directorate level of
Agriculture and Allied Department based on C-DAP/SAP. The Nodal department
compiled such proposals as Action/Annual Plan and placed before SLSC in violation
of RKVY guidelines.

Only DAP Kohima was prepared by obtaining the inputs from the members of the
District Agriculture and allied Committee and also inputs from Strategic Research
and Extension Plan (SREP) of ATMA. The Schemes prepared by the allied
departments were further compiled by the District Agriculture officer as per
guidelines and submitted to the Nodal department. In respect of preparation of DAPs
of other districts this procedure was not followed. Thus, it was evident that the
planning process was done at the district level only. The most important exercise in
the preparation of C-DAP i.e. the grass-root level planning (Village level plans and
Block level plans) was missing due to non existence of VAPU and BAPU which was
against the guidelines prescribed for preparation of District Agriculture Plan.

Further, the entire procedure of DAP preparation and approval was made without
involvement of the District Planning Committee/District Planning and Development
Boards (DPDB) in contravention with the roles and responsibilities of the District
Planning Committee as envisaged in Article 243ZD of the Constitution of India.

44


Chapter-II Economic Sector

2.4.6.2 Non-adherence of C-DAP with the format prescribed in C-DAP Manual

Chapter IV of the C-DAP Manual contains the format and the information to be
included in the DAP. Scrutiny of the DAPs revealed that none of the DAPs adhered to
the format for preparation of the C-DAP. The DAP was more of RKVY scheme
proposal than overall agricultural development plan of the district. Though the nodal
department had stated that Strategic Research Extension Plan (SREP) of Agricultural
Technology Management Agency (ATMA) referred to agro-climatic conditions,
availability of technology and natural resources, this report was not included in any of
the DAPs except Kohima district. Thus, the District Agriculture Plan could not
conform and include all necessary data as envisaged in C-DAP Manual.

The guidelines and minutes of the meeting of “National Conference on Convergence
in Agriculture Development” (December 2008) at Serial No. 15 (i), RKVY articulate
the need for convergence and integration of various programmes and additional
resources should be provided from RKVY wherever any other good scheme has
paucity of resources. All States decided to review the C-DAPs/SAP for factoring in
convergence with interventions in the rural sector. The plans would adopt the
methodology as per the C-DAP Manual brought out by the Planning Commission.
Comprehensive and convergent DAPs/SAP was expected from all States/UTs from
2009-10.

As per para 3.3 of C-DAP Manual circulated among the State representatives during
April 2008, resources available from various ongoing programmes such as NREGS,
BRGF, SGSY, Bharat Nirman, NHM, NFSM, NPCBB, NFDB, RKVY etc. should be
considered while preparing C-DAP.

However, no convergence programme with other Central and State programmes were
formulated in C-DAP/SAP. It was also observed that C-DAP did not take into
consideration the resources available from various ongoing programmes but the Plan
projections were prepared solely for RKVY Scheme.

The reply furnished by the Department (November 2013) was not relevant to the audit
observation.

2.4.6.3 No vision Statement included in the C-DAP

As per para 2.1 of C-DAP Manual of Planning Commission, Vision
document/Statement for development of a district over the next 10 to 15 years was an
essential pre-requisite for preparation of a C-DAP. Vision prepared at the district level
in consultation with all the stake holders would act as a guide to all concerned in
preparation of the DAP.

8 National Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme, Backward Region Grant Fund, Swarnajayanti
Gram Swarozgar Yojana, National Horticulture Mission, National Food Security Mission, National
Project for Cattle and Buffalo Breeding, National Fishery Development Board.

45


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

However, it was observed that no vision Statement was included in the C-DAP
prepared by the Agriculture and allied Departments and thus future growth and
development in the agriculture and allied sector could not be envisaged.

The Department while accepting the fact (November 2013) stated that though no
vision statement was included in the C-DAP, Perspective Plan for Agriculture
development of Nagaland with the objectives and strategies are mentioned in the
SAP.

2.4.6.4 Evaluation of C-DAP/SAP by the Additional Economic Advisor (AER)

As per directive of GOI, Ministry of Agriculture (MOA) (June 2009 and January
2010'°) C-DAP/SAP were to be evaluated by the designated Additional Economic
Advisor (AER) latest by 31 September 2009, for ensuring convergences and
comprehensiveness. However, evaluation was not done by the designated Additional
AERC/AERU, New Delhi. The State Agriculture Department stated that C-DAP/SAP
was not submitted to AERC/AERU for evaluation but the same was done by its
Statistical wing headed by a Jt. Director.

6.5 Preparation and submission of C-SAP to D.

The SAP was prepared by a consultancy firm based in Guwahati, Assam [M/s B.S
Consultant (Management and Project Consultants)] at a cost of 9.75 lakh and
submitted to the Ministry of Agriculture (DAC) for examination and approval in
December, 2009.

The selection of consultancy firm was not made by constituting a Committee at State
level or by short listing the agencies. The selection was made by the Directorate of
Agriculture without observing any selection criteria. In fact, the Nodal Department
had engaged the services of the consultant without the prior approval from the
Government.

As per scheme guidelines, the nodal agriculture department had to forward the final
report to the Department of Agriculture (DAC)/Planning Commission for
Examination and approval of SAP. It was observed that the DAC entrusted NIRD
Hyderabad to review the SAP. However, approval of the SAP was not available in the
records of the State agriculture department.

The State was required to place/upload DAPs and SAP on the website of the State
Agriculture Department (www.agringl.nic.in). However, it was observed that the
DAPs and SAP of the State were not uploaded/ hosted on their website. The
Department stated that due to network problem in the north eastern region, the
Ministry had verbally exempted them from doing so.

Further it was observed that SAP of the State was also not hosted on the Ministry of
Agriculture (DAC) RKVY website (www.rkvy.nic.in) which indicates that the
approved SAP was not available in case of GON.

Chapter-II Economic Sector

2.4.6.6 Activities proposed in C. SAP a: st the guidelines

Para 4.1 of the scheme guidelines listed the component/activities which would be
eligible for project based assistance under the Stream-I of the RKVY. The amount
under Stream-II would be available for strengthening the existing State sector
schemes and filling the resource gaps.

Scrutiny of DAPs and SAP revealed that there were activities/components proposed
which were not directly related to Agriculture and allied sectors and were not
permissible under RKVY. Activities such as renovation of office building, purchase
of vehicles, purchase of tractors, construction of new staff quarters, construction of
suspension bridges, procurement of furniture, construction of farmers hostel, training
hall, fencing of residential quarter, appointment of subject matter specialists, rubber,
tea plantation etc. though not permissible were included in DAP/SAP as detailed in
Appendix-2.4.1.

In reply (November 2013) the Nodal Department stated that the approach of bottom
up Planning was to take actual assessment of grass root needs and requirements in the
present context of the State. The Department further stated that implementation of
permissible activities as approved by the SLSC and incorporated in the action plan
were only taken up. The reply is not tenable as the scheme guidelines do not permit
inclusion of activities/components not directly related with Agriculture and allied
sectors.

2.4.6.7 Non-incorporation of project proposal in SAP

Though nine districts submitted project proposals under Department of Irrigation and
Flood Control and three districts under Department of Cooperation, the project
proposals were not incorporated in SAP. This indicates that the SAP did not envisage
the comprehensive projection of Agriculture and allied departments.

2.4.7 Audit Objective: Financial management ensured adequate, timely

availability of funds and their effective and economic utilisation.

The funding is 100 per cent central grant. During the 11" Plan period (2007-2012)
and 2012-13, GOI released हैं 16596 lakh to the State for implementation of 116

released to 18 projects and two sub-schemes were test checked as detailed in

which was 9.80 per cent of the projection made by GON as detailed in the table

As per para 7.1.5 of the guidelines the Department/Nodal Agency shall ensure that
Project-wise accounts are maintained by the implementing agencies and are subject to
the normal process of statutory audit. An inventory of the assets created under the
projects should be carefully prepared and assets that are no longer required should be
transferred to the Nodal Department, for its use and redeployment where possible.
However, neither project-wise separate accounts were maintained at any level (Nodal
department/Implementing departments) nor inventory of assets created was
maintained.

According to Rule 160 of the GFR, all government purchases should be made in a
transparent, competitive and fair manner. However, there was no competitive bidding
system and firms/suppliers were not empanelled by the Department/Government for
procurement. Out of the test checked departments (Agriculture, Horticulture and
Veterinary and Animal Husbandry), Veterinary and Animal Husbandry Department
forwarded supply orders without considering the credentials of the suppliers and
selected few suppliers were sent to the Government for approval, while Agriculture
and Horticulture Departments approved the selected few list of suppliers at the
Directorate level itself. Both at the Government and Directorate levels, reasons for
selection/rejection of suppliers for supply of materials such as saplings, fertilizers,
livestock, agri-tools etc. were not available. Thus, Departments did not adhere to
Financial Rules. There was no transparency in procurement and resultant weak
financial controls and management in the Veterinary and Animal Husbandry
department.

In reply (November 2013) the Nodal Department stated that procurement was made
based on the credentials of the suppliers/dealers approved at Directorate level.

The reply is not acceptable as the process of selection of suppliers as envisaged by
financial rules to ensure transparency, economy, efficiency and competitive rates were
not followed by the implementing Departments.
