Chapter-II Economic Sector

2.4.7.1 Receipt of grants and expenditure incurred (Stream 1 and Stream 11)

GON received an amount of € 16596.00 lakh from DAC for the period from 2007-08
to 2012-13 under Stream I and II, out of which an amount € 3650.00 lakh (25 per cent
of the total proposal of the State) was sanctioned directly by DAC for Stream-II
projects. However, the State had spent the entire amount under stream-I projects for
which they obtained the sanction of SLSC. Details of funds receipt and expenditure is
shown in Appendix-2.4.3. Perusal of the Appendix revealed that each year there were
un-spent balances and at the end of 2012-13 the accumulated unspent balance was
% 6665.03 lakh which resulted in delay in completion of the projects as detailed in
later paragraphs.

7.2 Delay in receipt of funds at various levels leading to blockade of funds

As per scheme guidelines, release of funds by the DAC depends on the sanction of
projects (Stream-l) by SLSC. The SLSC is responsible for sanctioning the projects
under Stream-1, monitoring/reviewing the progress and implementation of the
sanctioned projects/schemes as per guidelines, ensuring that no duplication of efforts
or resources takes place, commissioning/undertaking field studies, initiating
evaluation studies, undertaking any other projects of importance to the State, ensuring
no inter-disparities with financial matters and shall meet as often as required but shall
meet at least once in a quarter.

The project under RKVY addresses agriculture production and productivity in the
rain-fed areas of the State and is extremely important that full scale implementation of
projects take place in the Kharif season itself. Any delay in release of funds to the
implementing departments had a cascading effect and if the States do not carry out
Kharif crops based interventions; the loss cannot be made good in the remaining part
of the year.

GON released funds to the Implementing departments directly instead of routing
through nodal department during three years (2007-08 to 2009-10). From 2010-11
onwards GON released the funds through nodal department. The delay in release of
funds for the first three years to implementing departments ranged from one to 14
months from the date of DAC release. Further, there was also a delay from one to
nine months in releasing the funds to the implementing departments from the GON
through nodal department for the years 2010-11, 2011-12 and 2012-13 as detailed in
Appendix 2.4.4. Besides these delays, the funds were also kept in the Civil Deposit
(CD) for almost six months during the year 2011-12. In 2012-13, out of हैं 8575 lakh
released by GOI an amount of % 2588 lakh kept under Civil Deposit was not released
till 31.08.2013.

Had the nodal department and implementing departments adhered to the calendar of
activities as per the departmental guidelines the delay in release of funds could have
been avoided. Besides, the delay in release of funds affected the farmers in losing out
two seasons of crop cultivation (Kharif and Rabi) especially during the year 2008-09
and non-achievement of the target set.

49


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

The Department in reply (November 2013) stated that the accumulated unspent
balance was due to delay of release of funds from the State Government. However,
project implementation commenced in anticipation of receipt of funds.

implications on projects

As per scheme guidelines, the State Government was to notify the SLSC within a
month after the publication of the scheme guidelines (August 2007). The SLSC was
notified for constitution of committee by the State Government towards the end of
2007-08 after a delay of four and half months.

During the year 2007-08 and 2008-09 there was short release of % 626.00 lakh and
% 694 lakh respectively by GOI. The reason for short release during 2007-08 was due
to the fact that SLSC did not meet since its inception to accord sanction for the
projects under Stream-I. During 2008-09, the project under Stream-I was accorded
sanction by SLSC only in January 2009. Hence, GOI released an amount % 596.00
lakh as 17 instalment in February 2009 and did not release the second instalment
amounting to हैं. 694 lakh. Due to short release of funds, the Department lost one or
more crop seasons and target set to be achieved for that year could not be fulfilled.

The Department in reply (November 2013) stated that the delay occurred due to

5 Details of expenditure incurred out of 1 per cent share of RKVY funds

The scheme guidelines permit the State to use upto one per cent of its total RKVY
funds for incurring administrative expenditure that includes payments to consultants,
recurring expenses of various kind, staff costs, etc. However, no permanent
employment can be created, nor can vehicles be purchased.

The Ministry of Agriculture in 2009 also instructed the State Government that
manpower hiring, hiring of vehicles, stationery, computer, POL, TA/DA and other
consumables are not to be made part of the project proposal. These items are required
to be charged from the available administrative funds with the approval of the SLSC.

However, on scrutiny it was observed that the implementing departments were
allotted funds directly by the State for the year 2007-08 to 2009-10 instead of routing
through nodal department. No records for deduction/utilisation of one per cent
administrative expenses could be furnished by the implementing departments. But
from 2010-11 onwards the nodal department deducted 1 per cent of total RKVY
funds amounting % 106.30 lakh (% 6.30, = 29.00 and हैं 71.00 lakh), details of
expenditure incurred are given in Appendix -.2.4.5.

Agriculture Department incurred an amount of = 18.00 lakh towards monitoring and
evaluation, documentation of RKVY and meeting/conference. However, supporting
documents such as monitoring and evaluation report, documentation of RKVY and
minutes of meeting etc. were not available. Further, an amount of ~ 31.00 lakh had
not been utilised as on 31-03-2013 out of € 106.30 lakh. It was also observed that no
administrative funds were allotted to the implementing departments.

The Nodal department in reply (November 2013) stated that supporting documents in
the form of cash memos, APRs and list of trainings had been produced to audit.

The reply was not tenable as supporting documents such as monitoring and evaluation
report, documentation of RKVY and minutes of meeting were not furnished to audit.

51



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

It was also noticed that Horticulture Department in 2011-12 spent an amount of € 3.18
lakh towards procurement of computers and accessories out of हैं 298 lakh provided
for “Area expansion of Horticulture crop’ though this expenditure was to be met from
the Administrative cost (one per cent of the total project cost) deducted by the nodal
department. This resulted in short execution of projects to that extent.

The Department in reply (November 2013) stated that as per section 3.6 of the
guidelines the Department had deducted one per cent out of the total allocation during
the year 2011-12 and utilised it for procurement of stationery, computers, copiers, etc.

The reply is not tenable as the Nodal Department had already deducted one per cent
from the total State allocation.

2.4.7.7 Submission of Utilisation Certificates

Nodal department had submitted the Utilisation Certificates (UCs) for the entire funds
released by DAC. However, scrutiny of UCs revealed that:

> No funds were released by the State Government during 2007-08. Even then
an amount of % 2.09 crore released by DAC was shown as utilised by the State
Government.

> There was a huge cash balance at the end of each financial year as per Cash
Books of the implementing Departments as detailed in Appendix 2.4.3 but
UCs were furnished to DAC for the entire amount.

> During 2011-12 and 2012-13, an amount of % 18.77 crore and & 25.88 crore
was kept in Civil Deposit (CD) up to August 2012 and till March 2013
respectively. However, UCs for the same were furnished.

> There was no system of submission of UCs from field offices of the selected
departments except in respect of Veterinary and Animal Husbandry
Department.

To conclude, GON was issuing UC to the DAC on the basis of money released to
implementing departments instead of actual utilisation of funds on the projects.

The Nodal Department in reply (November 2013) stated that once the funds were
released by the Government, UCs were sent to the Union Ministry without actual

Implementation of various projects under agriculture and allied departments was
delayed for six to 14 months due to delay in SLSC sanction and late release of funds
by the GON. No project was implemented during the year 2007-08. The projects
implemented during the year 2008-09, 2009-10 and 2010-11 were spread out thinly

Agriculture and allied departments started implementing the scheme in Compact Area
mode’’ from 2011-12. The main objective was to have a visible impact of
implementation of various programmes under RKVY. The identification and selection
of the Compact Area project was done by ATMA (Agriculture Technology
Management Agency) under the chairmanship of Deputy Commissioner.

After introduction of compact area system there was better coordinated efforts
between the agriculture and allied departments. It had helped in better monitoring and
evaluation of the projects and also identifying the beneficiaries/farmers. However,
there were no modalities for selection of beneficiaries either in DAPs/SAP or in
Annual Action Plan for the departments (Agriculture and Horticulture). There were no
pre-selected beneficiaries before distribution of inputs. The selection of beneficiaries
and distribution of inputs was delegated to village council/RKVY member without
prescribing any criteria in case of Agriculture Department. While Horticulture
Department selected beneficiaries through district officers concerned, Department of
Veterinary and Animal Husbandry as per DAPs and Annual Action Plan (2011-12),
constituted a Committee for selection of beneficiaries/target groups from selected
villages/towns consisting of District Veterinary Officer (DVO) as Chairman, Village
Council Chairman, Village Development Board (VDB) Secretary, representative from
the Directorate as members and Veterinary Surgeon (VAS) as member secretary. The
beneficiaries’ selection at the grass-root level was done by the Village Council. The
selected beneficiaries as approved by the concerned District Veterinary Officers
(DVOs) were sent to the Directorate which in turn forwarded the same to the
Government for final approval.

It is evident from the above that the Department of Veterinary and Animal Husbandry
constituted a Committee for selection of beneficiaries/target groups from selected

rement through un-authorised dealer

The Fertilizer (Control) Order (FCO) 1985 requires that “Every person including a
manufacturer, an importer, a pool handling agency, wholesaler and a retail dealer
intending to sell or offer for sale or carrying on the business of selling of fertilizer
shall make a Memorandum of Intimation in form “A1” to the Notified Authority as
per the terms and conditions specified therein alongwith the Certificate of Source in
form “O” of FCO alongwith the fees prescribed by the concerned State Government.
Every Certificate of registration and every Authorisation letter shall, unless renewed,
suspended or cancelled, shall be valid for a period of three years from the date of
issue. The Certificate of Source was required in case of control items. Only three
fertilizers, namely urea, zincated urea and anhydrous ammonia were under the price
control items.

Further clause 11(5) of FCO requires that “If an application for renewal of a
Certificate of registration or authorisation letter was not made within one month from
the date of expiry of their period of validity, the same shall be deemed to have lapsed
on the date on which its validity expired and any business carried on after that shall be
deemed to have been carried on in contravention of clause 7 of the Fertilizer (Control)
Order”.

The Department issued supply orders to M/s Farmer Enterprises, Kohima during
2008-09 and M/s L. Duolo and Co, Kohima during 2009-10. Scrutiny of records
revealed that the dealer M/s Farmer Enterprises, Kohima was registered with the
Agriculture Department during 1992 but had not renewed their registration since then.
Their Certificate of Source for control items was not furnished. The registration of
dealer M/s L. Duolo and Co, Kohima had expired on 26-08-2008 and they had not
renewed their registration since then. The Certificate of Source for Control items
revealed that the M/s Assam Fertilizer Corporation (SSI unit), Guwahati Assam

55


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

authorised M/s L. Duolo and Co to deal in selling their products with effect from
10-06-2011. Since the dealers were deemed unauthorised due to non-renewal of
registration, the supply orders issued by the Department were irregular.

The Department in reply (November 2013) stated that the Department issued supply
order to the dealer despite non-renewal of the dealership license as there were no
other dealers dealing with fertilizers. The Department however, assured that the
suppliers dealership license shall be renewed as advised.

The reply is not factually true and therefore, not acceptable as two other dealers M/s
R.L Nelson, Dimapur and M/s Indian Potash Limited, Nagaland were also registered
with the Agriculture Department during 2008 to 2010.

(14 weeks) for Farmers Field ool (F.

FFS was a season long programme which was organised in farmer’s field by meeting
once in a week. It was
(seeding to harvest) (14-15 weeks) so that it covers all the different developmental
stages of the crop and their related management practices. The FFS consists of a
group of 30 farmers and 5 agriculture extension officers. Bench Mark Survey was to
be conducted in the village selected for FFS before commencement of the programme
so as to assess the pest problems, level of pesticide usage, varieties grown, size of
holding and the social and economic level of the farmers. Tentative programme/action
plan for 14 consecutive weeks was to be prepared incorporating all the curriculum
activities of IPM. In the FFS, farmers conduct a study comparing IPM strategy with
common farmer’s practices. They should have an IPM and a Farmer’s Practice plot

ason long and should last for at least one cropping season

(Non-IPM). At each demonstration site, the facilitator/trainer should maintain a record
of all the activities including yield and record in the register, the data of which will be
used to work out cost benefit ratio to compare IPM and Non-IPM field. Farmer’s
Field Day was to be organised after 14 weeks of training wherein detailed discussions
among the trainee farmers and the trainer takes place on various aspects including the
success story as well as on the points of failures.

During 2008-09, DAO Zunheboto and Mon were allotted three FFS each (including
backlog implemented during 2009-10) and an amount of हैं 0.46 lakh was spent by
each district on conduct of season long training programme (14 weeks) FFS at
Sheyipu, Asukhomi, Yemishe (Zunheboto) and Techa, Temong, Tesha (Mon) villages
respectively.

Scrutiny of attendance register of DAO, Zunheboto revealed that number of weekly
demonstration/training ranged from 3 to 5 weeks against the target of 14 weeks. No
attendance register for FSS was maintained by DAO, Mon. However, IPM payment
register revealed that the number of weekly demonstration/training for each FFS was
10 weeks against the target of 14 weeks. Thus, the FFS did not cover all the different
developmental stages of the crop and their related management practices due to non-
conduct of weekly (14 weeks) demonstration/training throughout the cropping season
(seeding to harvest).

2.4.8.9 Abandoned Project

Some of the important factors which determine the success or failure of Cash crops
cultivation are agro-climatic condition, quality of the planting material, market
demand of the proposed product, marketing facilities, communication, post harvest
management facilities etc. Proper feasibility study of the proposed project/site is a
prerequisite before project implementation.

During 2008-09 under “Integrated Development of Major Horticulture Crops”, three
beneficiaries in Phusto village, Dimapur district were provided 2,28,000 Pineapple
suckers, 95 Hand Gloves and हैं 4.94 lakh as land development/maintenance cost.
However, the project could not be physically verified as the villagers had abandoned
pineapple cultivation during 2012. The Department conveyed the information of the
Village chief of Phusto that the pineapple projects were abandoned in 2012 as the
approach road to Phusto village was not motorable during monsoon and therefore the
harvest/produce from the village could not be transported to the market.

Thus, failure on the part of the Department to undertake feasibility study of the
project site before implementation of the project in an inaccessible area and improper
selection of beneficiaries led to abandonment of the projects.

The Department in reply (November 2013) stated that they could not foresee that the
projects would be abandoned as those projects were successfully implemented during
the initial years. With regard to conduct of feasibility study, the Department assured
that henceforth, they would ensure that such lapses do not re-occur.

2.4.9 Audit Objective: Internal control mechanisms were put in place to ensure

efficient and effective monitoring and control over implementation;
monitoring mechanism at each level was adequate.

The National Institute of Rural Development (NIRD), Hyderabad was appointed by
the Ministry as Consultant for monitoring and evaluation of Rashtriya Krishi Vikas
Yojana. NIRD conducted the monitoring and evaluation of RKVY projects during
August 2010 and comments and suggestions were forwarded to the GON for
corrective action. However, till August 2013 no corrective action as suggested by
NIRD were taken by the Government.

As per guidelines, SLSC was responsible for monitoring/review of implementation of
projects and evaluation of the same from time to time. SLSC held only six meetings
against the target of 24 meetings during the period of audit for sanctioning of the
projects only. However, no review meetings on the implementation of the projects
(financial/physical progress) were held by SLSC during the period covered as stated
by the nodal Department.

As per Para 6.2 of the scheme guidelines, the quorum for the SLSC meeting would
not be complete without the presence of at least one representative from the

62


Chapter-II Economic Sector

Government of India. However, it was observed from the meeting minutes of SLSC
that no representative from the Government of India attended the SLSC meeting held
on 10-04-2008 and 12-01-2009 in contravention of the guidelines.

It was also noticed that lack of monitoring at SLSC/APC level was pointed out by the
third party monitoring report submitted by the NIRD. Also, it stated that there was no
information about reviews conducted at Government and nodal officer level. Despite
that no corrective action was taken by the Department/Government.

There was no Committee set up under APC (Agriculture Production Committee)
exclusively for RKVY to review the scheme implementation. However, an “Inter
Departmental Working Group” (IDWG) under the chairmanship of APC constituted
under Agriculture Technology Management Agency (ATMA) was responsible to
review the scheme implementation. Against 72 meetings to be held only seven
meetings of APC were conducted which indicate lack of coordination between
various implementing departments.

There was no system put in place at any level for monitoring/field visit and
Evaluation of RKVY projects in the State. It was also noticed in audit that the nodal
department conducted review of implementation of RKVY projects by other
implementing departments only thrice during the last six years. Result of the same
was not known due to non-documentation.

1 Implementation of web: ed management information

MIS was put into operation to ensure transparency and accuracy of data relating to the
scheme and also to provide ready reference of records in the form of reports whenever
required for monitoring, evaluation and planning of programmes. One of the
important aims of MIS was to collect and disseminate relevant information and data
related to each project and also to collect progress and completion details of each
project over its life cycle.

It was noticed that the State/Nodal Department did not establish IT based MIS in the
first year of its implementation as per the scheme guidelines. The system started only
from 2010-11 onwards.

It was also noticed that the State did not attend RKVY- Data-base Management and
information System (RDMIS) training conducted by the ministry during 2010-11
which had affected the smooth functioning of the MIS operation to some extent.

2.

9.2 Anal MIS data

Analysis of MIS data and physical data of the selected projects revealed physical data
did not match with web-based data as detailed in Appendix 2.4.11. It was also
noticed that even in respect of completed projects many important fields viz. date of
completion, actual output, growth impact, etc. were left blank without data entry in
the MIS'®. MIS did not capture the completion details of each project over its life

19 Source: Report Menu: Year-wise Approved Project Details from Ministry website www.rkvy.nic.in

63


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

cycle due to non-updation of data in RKVY website of DAC. There was no in-built
control to avoid blank data entry of important fields for completed projects.

2.4.10 Audit Objective: The objectives of maximising returns to the farmers in

Agriculture and allied sectors were achieved and the State could achieve four per

cent growth in the agricultural sector during the XIth Plan period.

During the SLSC meeting (August 2012) the Agriculture Production Commissioner
(APC) pointed out that the Agriculture and allied departments need to work out the
contribution made to the GSDP separately through implementation of RKVY
programme in the State. However, no efforts had been made to determine the
contributions made to the GSDP. Besides, no effective mechanism had been put in
place to evaluate the impact of the projects specifically under RKVY scheme. As a
result, audit could not conclusively determine and assess the output of the scheme
with respect to 4 per cent annual growth in agriculture sector.

However, it was seen that the overall average annual growth of Agriculture and allied
sectors for 11" plan was 5.19 per cent which was an excess of 1.19 per cent above
National target growth rate. The Agriculture Department alone had achieved 6.14 per
cent agricultural growth rate during the oh plan. The State was awarded the “Krishi
Karman Award 2011-12 for best State under Total Food-grains Production (Category-
IUD”. The schemes under RKVY were being implemented by nine Departments and
the expenditure was thinly spread over the State. Thus, audit could not conclude
whether the implementation of RKVY projects contributed to increase of agricultural
growth rate in the State.

2.4.11 Conclusion

The RKVY scheme was implemented initially by seven agriculture and allied
departments and from 2012-13 two more departments (Irrigation and flood control
and Co-operation) were included. The project implementation was practically started
from 2008-09 though GOI had released funds during 2007-08 to the State. The State
prepared C-SAP out of C-DAP of all the districts. There were many anomalies in C-
DAP/SAP which needs to be addressed. Delay and in-adequate SLSC meetings
resulted in delay in release of funds from GOI. There was also delay in release of
funds from the State to implementing Departments and nodal department which
resulted in delay in project implementation. There was no competitive bidding system
followed for procurements. Undue benefits were given to select few suppliers and
contractors. During project implementation, points such as short/non-supply of
materials, wasteful expenditure, non-achievement of target, irregularities in supply of
benefits, excess payment to suppliers and abandonment of projects were noticed.
Proper mechanism to monitor or evaluate the contribution of production at State level
did not exist. Also at the department levels, proper mechanism for post monitoring
and evaluation of the projects was absent which resulted in failure to assess the
success of the projects.

64


Chapter-II Economic Sector

Though the State of Nagaland achieved an overall average annual growth of 5.19 per
cent (an excess of 1.19 per cent) above national target during 117 plan in Agriculture
sectors, audit could not determine whether the implementation of RKVY projects had
contributed to increase of agricultural growth rate in the State.

2.4.12 Recommendations

> Planning should be bottom-up approach so as to ensure its ownership by all
stakeholders. Prioritisation of projects should be done during sanctioning to
allocate resources for addressing all the objectives.

> SLSC meetings should be held timely as per the guidelines so as to avoid
delay in release of funds from the Ministry. Calendar of activities in the
common guidelines may be followed.

> Streamline the release of funds from the State to Nodal Department and from
nodal to implementing departments. UCs should be submitted as per actual
utilisation of funds.

> To ensure transparency, economy, efficiency and competitive rates, all
procurements should be done after notice inviting tenders (NIT) or from
Government empanelled vendors.

> Projects should be implemented by all stakeholders in efficient, economical
and effective manner so as to avoid short/non-achievement of targets.

> State Level, district level and Grass-root level supervision, monitoring and
evaluation needs to be strengthened as prescribed in the guidelines to achieve
the desired outcomes.

PLANNING AND CO-ORDINA' IN DEPARTMENT

lementation of Local Area Development Programme in Nagaland

With a view to having decentralisation of planning process at the grass root level
Nagaland Local Area Development Scheme was introduced in 1993-94. The name of
the programme was changed to Local Area Development Programme (LADP) from
the annual plan 2004-05 covering all the 60 assembly constituencies with the
objective of taking up of developmental programmes in critical areas through mass

participation of people and to create durable community assets particularly in the

pockets which are relatively less developed.

The constituency wise allocation in a year had been enhanced to % 1.00 crore during
2009-10 from % 75 lakh in the 60 Assembly constituencies in the State.

The Performance Audit on Local Area Development Programme (LADP) in Nagaland
was conducted to review the systems adopted by the Departments and the efforts of
the State Government to ascertain whether objectives of the scheme were met in
economical, efficient and effective manner.

65


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

Highlights

Instead of decentralising the entire planning process by involving the Village
Development Boards in identification of the projects, the Department mostly relied on
the recommendations of the elected members. This not only resulted in duplication of
projects but also defeated the objective of the planning mechanism.

(Paragraph-2.5.8.4)
Though Technical estimates of the projects were prepared, it was seen that those
estimates were either incomplete or deficient indicating that the technical estimates
were prepared without appropriate inputs.

(Paragraphs-2.5.8.5)

Joint physical verification of projects in 15 Constituencies revealed that 146 projects
taken up at a cost of हैं 5.18 crore during 2008-2013 were non-existent indicating
possible misappropriation to that extent. Besides, overlapping of four works valued at
र₹ 0.73 crore under Roads and Bridges were also noticed.

(Paragraphs-2.5.10.4, 2.5.10.6 & 2.5.10.8)

Co-ordination between the Nodal Department and the implementing
Department was deficient. Monitoring and evaluation was also not effective.

(Paragraph-2.5.11.1)

2.5.1 Introduction

2.5.1.1 The Scheme

According to the LADP guidelines of the State, the funds were to be utilised in four
categories of scheme viz, individual Base Scheme, Community Project, Augmentation
and Employment Promotion Project and Common Pool Scheme to develop critical
areas in various sectors by decentralised planning to involve mass participation of
people at grass root level in order to create durable assets and income and
employment generating projects in 60 Assembly constituencies in the State.

Individual Base Schemes: Individual Base Schemes restricted to 20 per cent of the
total constituency wise outlay was in the nature of Grants-in-Aid and Swarnajayanti
Gram Swarojgar Yojana (SGSY) schemes of Rural Development Department.

Community Project: Community project were mandatory for implementation of
Major Community Projects with a minimum of 35 per cent of constituency wise
allocation upto 2009-10 and the projects categorised under this programme should be
completed within a maximum period of two years. The sector allocation was
enhanced to 40 per cent since July 2010.

Augmentation and Employment Promotion Project: This category was to cover
income and self-employment generation or the programme already initiated by the
Developmental department but could not be completed due to paucity of funds. Under
this programme 20 per cent of allocation of constituency was earmarked for assisting

66


Chapter-II Economic Sector

groups of un-employed professionals also. The sector allocation was enhanced to 25
per cent since July 2010 from 20 per cent.

Common Pool Scheme: Under Common Pool Fund a minimum of 25 per cent in
each constituency was earmarked in the District/Sub-Divisional Planning and
Development Board (DPDB/SDPDB) for formulating schemes of the under privileged
section of people and neglected areas which are identified by the chairman of DPDB.
The sector allocation was reduced to 15 per cent since July 2010.

The Planning and Coordination Department, Government of Nagaland is responsible
for implementation and monitoring of the scheme at the State level. The District
Planning Officers (DPOs) are the nodal officers at the district level under the
chairmanship of Deputy Commissioner to monitor the scheme implementation at
districts.

Organisational setup both functional and financial:

Scope of Audit

The Performance Audit covered the period from 2008-09 to 2012-2013 through test
check of records of the Development Commissioner, Department of Planning and
Co-ordination, 4 Deputy Commissioners out of 11, four DPOs out of 11 during April
to August 2013.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

2.5.4 Audit Objectives

The broad objectives of the performance audit were to assess whether:

« the planning process was adequately devised to create durable community
assets by formulating appropriate schemes to fill the developmental gaps.

« adequate funds were released in a timely manner and utilised for specific
purposes.

¢ the projects were executed efficiently, effectively and economically to achieve
the intended objectives and;

¢ the monitoring mechanism with the Department was effective.

Audit Criteria

The main criteria used for the performance audit were:
* Scheme guidelines and instructions issued by the State
Government/Commissioner and Planning Commission of India.
¢ General Financials Rules.
e Technical estimates approved for implementation of projects.
¢ Provisions of agreements governing execution and maintenance of assets.

6 Audit Methodology

The performance audit began with an entry conference (22 April 2013) with
management of the State Government to discuss the audit objectives and scope of
Performance Audit in detail. Audit findings were communicated to the management
and a presentation made on the findings which were also discussed in an exit
conference held on 12 September 2013. The replies of the Department furnished in
September 2013 have duly been incorporated in the Report at appropriate places.

Audit methodology was further based on:

6) Audit sampling

Minimum of 25 per cent districts (four districts” out of eleven districts) to scrutinise
the records of Deputy Commissioners and DPOs have been selected based on
Probability Proportional to Size (PPS) without replacement method of sampling.
Within the selected districts minimum of 25 per cent (a total of 15 constituencies” out
of 60 in the State) and 25 per cent of projects were selected on Simple Random
Sampling Without Replacement (SRSWOR) method.

AUDIT FINDINGS
Audit Objective - 1

2.5.8 Whether the planning process was adequately devised to create durable
community assets by formulating appropriate schemes to fill the

developmental gaps.

Selection and compilation of LADP projects at district level was scheduled to be
finalised by 30 July and the selected projects by the District Planning and
Development Board (DPDB) was to be forwarded to Planning and Co-ordination
Department by 16 August every year. Examination and approval by the State
Planning Board (SPB) ought to be completed by 10 September in order to issue the
Administrative approval of GON by 20 September every year followed by the
implementation of scheme.

1 Constitution of Developmental Boards at

The Planning and Development Boards at State as well as at district levels were
constituted in 2005 to select, compile and approve the LADP projects according to the
date line fixed in the Calendar of Action for implementation of LADP. The District
Planning Boards headed by Chairman” constituted at districts should be the Nodal

2 Elected member of Legislative Assembly

69


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

Agencies to scrutinise the schemes to ensure the strict conformity as well as to
coordinate and supervise the implementation of LADP schemes in consultation with
qualified technical person drawn from Civil Administrative Works Division
(CAWD)/District Rural development Agency (DRDA) or from the allied Rural
Development Department. The DPO was assigned to forward the selected projects to
the Department of Planning and Co-ordination for scrutiny and compilation after the
approval of the DPDB. The compiled projects shall be examined by the State
Planning Board (SPB) headed by Hon’ble Chief Minister before its approval.

Technically qualified personnel were required to render technical and expert guidance
at various stages of project implementation. However, scrutiny revealed that the
services of the technical experts were limited to preparation of estimates and
verification/certifying the project completion in order to release payments to the
beneficiaries. Thus, the technical guidance was not provided during the time of
project implementation to improve the quality of developmental activities.

The DPO, Dimapur while accepting the audit observation stated (September 2013)
that technically qualified services were not utilised in some implemented schemes.
The DPO, Mon and Tuensang replied (September 2013) that technical personnel
rendered guidance during the various stages of the implementation of the scheme in
co-ordination with DPO. But the fact remained that technical personnel was not
involved during the implementation of the scheme. Thus, services of the technical
experts were limited to preparation of estimates and certification of projects
completion only.

5.8.2 Village profile

The District Planning and Development Boards (DPDBs) would prepare village
profile of every village by obtaining data through the concerned Departments with the
help of Department of Economics and Statistics, Geographical Information System
and National Informatics Centre network to identify critical areas to bridge
developmental gaps to eradicate poverty in the villages and these data need to be
updated every year to include the achievements of the previous year.

It was noticed in audit that the village profile was not created to identify the critical
areas wherein development were required in the four test-checked districts. Also,
district profile available in the district annual plan did not disclose the missing
developments in order to bridge the developmental gaps in the less developed areas to
eradicate poverty.

In the absence of village profile, the projects were selected randomly by elected
members and due to the deficiencies in the haphazard selection of projects by the
elected members, many projects were not executed and overlapping of the projects
were also noticed in audit as discussed in Paragraphs 2.5.10.4 and 2.5.10.6.

DPO, Dimapur and Mon accepted (September 2013) audit findings and the DPO,
Kohima and Tuensang were silent on the facts.

70


Chapter-II Economic Sector

2.5.8.3 Calendar of a

The Department of Planning and Coordination was assigned to frame the calendar of
Action Plan every year to append various stages for selection, compilation,
preparation of Technical estimates, approval from DPDB/SPB, clearance of SPB,
issue of expenditure sanction, issue of work order, verification, release of funds,
handing over the assets created to the user groups with responsibility for maintenance,
etc.

Though the calendar of Action Plan was framed by the Department every year,
criteria and time schedule for selection of beneficiaries to award the work order and
the time schedule for handing over of the assets to the user groups with responsibility
for maintenance were not incorporated in the action plan. As a result, the beneficiaries
were selected without following any criteria and the assets created through LADP
scheme were not handed over to the user groups for maintenance.

The DPO, Dimapur while accepting the facts (September 2013) stated that the
planning process would be streamlined. DPO, Kohima and Tuensang were silent on
audit findings.

4 Selection and compilation of

Para 6 (a) of the guidelines for district plan issued by the Planning Commission, GOI
prioritised (August 2008) LADP programme on need basis as well as setting the goals
for a five year period and the draft plan of projects. This programme should emerge
from either Gramsabha/Village Development Board or Panchayat /Village Council
level. Guidelines of LADP further envisage that every recognised village should be
covered in LADP and projects identified on priority basis in the village should be
discussed in open meetings. Heads of the development Departments in the districts
should submit Annual Works Programme/Action Plan for information and reference
of the members with a view to avoid duplication and overlapping of the schemes.

Implementing agency (DPO) at district level should select and compile the projects
out of the draft plan prepared by the Gramsabha/VDB or Panchayat/VC according to
need and set the goals. Scrutiny of records of four DPOs revealed that such draft plans
neither emerged from Village Development Boards nor from Village Councils.
Instead, all the projects catogorised under community scheme, individual scheme and
augmentation and employments promotion projects were included only as per the
recommendations made by the elected members from the respective Legislature
Assembly (constituencies) in the Annual Plans of LADP programme during 2008-09
to 2012-13. The common pool projects were selected by the chairman of the DPDB.
The fact of non-involvement of Village Development Board or Village Council in
identification of projects was confirmed in the beneficiary survey.

Further, the heads of the departments at district level did not furnish works
programme to discuss the concerned works programme in detail to avoid duplication
of works, in the DPDB meeting.

71


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

Due to the selection of projects by the elected members instead of originating through
the Gramsabha/VDB to bridge the developmental gaps by involving the people at
grass-root to create durable community assets the objectives of the entire planning
process of the programme was defeated.

Scrutiny further revealed following deficiencies in planning process:

e Heads of the development departments neither furnished any works
programmes to DPDB nor discussed in the meeting to avoid duplication of
works.

* Nodal officer, LADP programme did not collect annual works plan/action plan
from other allied development departments.

Instead. of decentralising the planning process by involving the Gramsabha/Village
Development Boards in identification of projects selection and identification of
projects were done on the basis of recommendation by the elected members. Further,
duplication of project implementation was noticed as discussed in Paragraph 2.5.10.6
due to non-observance of compilation of works programme of the allied departments
by the DPDB.

The DPO, Dimapur while accepting the facts (September 2013) stated that steps
would be taken to avoid duplication and overlapping of schemes in future. The DPO,
Kohima was silent on audit findings and the replies furnished by the DPO, Tuensang
and Mon were not relevant to the audit findings.

port the calendar of action plan.

Technical estimate of the list of projects under the LADP should be prepared in order
to assess the technical feasibility of the project as well as to propose the fund required
for each project. Technical person drawn from CAWD/DRDA or from the allied
Department were entrusted for preparation of technical estimates. The technical
estimate so prepared by the experts would be scrutinised by the DBDP while
finalising the project at the district level. The final list of works approved by the
DPDB would be forwarded to Department of Planning and Co-ordination for
approval. Technical estimates prepared by the expert group should normally be
endorsed with history/background of the project, name of the village, location, nature
and scope of development of the project along with socio economic progress to be
achieved in support of sectoral lay out, project summary of quantum of work,
quantities along with material and labour cost separately in terms of financial
analysis, schedule of execution of projects etc.

Clause 9 of the guidelines, further, stipulated the purchase of materials by the DPDB
in the case of construction/building projects wherein material components were
involved. Planning and Coordination Department standardised funding scale of
schemes under LADP in respect of 43 items of model schemes under individual based
schemes.

72


Chapter-II Economic Sector

The technical experts should prepare estimates considering all the aforementioned
facts while preparing the technical estimate for proper utilisation of funds to create
durable assets to fill developmental gaps in the village.

Test check of 430 technical estimates prepared and approved for implementation of
LADP projects out of 3185 Technical estimates in 15 Assembly constituencies
revealed following deficiencies:

« Specific location of the projects to be implemented was not available in the
technical estimates

¢ Technical estimates neither disclosed the background nor the history of the
required projects to describe the intended benefit out of the projects.

e Sectoral layout of the project was not prepared to support the technical
estimates

« Technical estimates were not supported by detailed estimates. Instead the
estimates were prepared to accommodate the allocated LADP funds applying
the Schedule of rates applicable from time to time.

e The technical estimates were not approved as per the financial powers
delegated to the competent authorities.

e Technical estimates did not provide separate data for material cost to be
procured and issued to the work by DPDB.

« Technical estimates were not prepared in accordance to the standardised
funding scale of schemes in respect of 43 items of model schemes under
individual based schemes.

Thus, the technical estimates prepared and approved were incomplete or deficient
indicating that the technical estimates were prepared without appropriate inputs.

Due to the lack of aforementioned data available in the technical estimates for the
LADP, location of work, achievement of socio-economic progress, quality of work
executed, quantum of labour component and material component involved in the
project, etc. could not be analysed in audit.

In addition to this, in the absence of above particulars, the location of the project
executed, component or the part of the infrastructure assisted through LADP and non-
execution of projects could not be ascertained in audit. Inadequate technical estimates
also resulted in short execution, diversion of projects, overlapping of projects as
discussed in Paragraphs 2.5.10.4, 2.5.10.5 and 2.5.10.6.

DPO, Dimapur and Mon while accepting the facts (September 2013) stated that steps
would be taken to streamline the process of preparation of technical estimates. The
DPO, Kohima and Tuensang were silent on audit findings.

73


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

2.5.8.6 Irrelevancy of Technical estimates

As per the calendar of action plan, preparation of technical estimates should be
completed by July to commence the work by September every year. However,
scrutiny of technical estimates (150) approved during 2011-13 (two years) in
Tuensang district revealed that the proposal for preparation of technical estimates was
forwarded to Assistant Engineer, CAWD in February 2012 and January 2013 against
the stipulated dateline of July 2011 and July 2012 whereas the DPO, Tuensang issued
work orders as per the timeframe (October 2011 & 2012) and also submitted the
progress reports in November 2011 & 2012 after the verification of 50 per cent of
projects by the Verification Committee in order to confer release of funds to the
beneficiaries. This corroborated that the technical estimates were prepared even after
the release of funds to the beneficiaries and the technical estimates were only a
formality to get the LADP funds.

The DPO, Tuensang while accepting the facts (September 2013) stated that the
weather condition, lack of proper infrastructure and time bound nature of works
compelled them to prepare the technical estimate at later date in certain cases and
assured that corrective measures would be taken to avoid such discrepancies in future.

.7 Clearance from Dist Planning and Development Board (DPDB)

DPDBs were constituted since 2005 under the chairmanship of Members of
Legislative Assembly (MLA), Deputy Commissioners as Vice-Chairman and DPOs
as member in the four test checked districts to scrutinise the projects selected
followed by its clearance, issue of work orders and nominating the verification
committee for project verification. As per the calendar of action plan, DPDB had to
meet at least thrice in a year to scrutinise and approve (August) the projects selected
for the year concerned, issue (September) the work orders and certify the completion
(February) of projects pertaining to the year concerned.

Scrutiny of the records of four test checked DPOs revealed that the committee in each
sampled district met once a year to discuss the LADP activities against the norms of
at least thrice in a year. Thus, there was a shortfall in conducting DPDB meetings in
all the test checked districts by 67 per cent every year.

The DPO, Dimapur while accepting the facts (September 2013) stated that the DPDB
meets and approves schemes in a single sitting. DPO, Kohima, Tuensang were silent
on audit findings and the DPO, Mon stated that DPDB reviewed the LADP projects
during all the monthly meetings. However, nothing in this regard was found in the
minutes of the monthly meeting of DPDB of Mon district.

. Community projects were mandatory and funds had to be utilised for
implementation of Major Community Projects with a minimum of 35 per cent of
constituency wise allocation upto 2009-10 which was revised to 40 per cent and such
projects categorised under this programme should be completed within a maximum
period of 2 years. It was noticed that Department expended 41 per cent of total
constituency allocation on community projects upto 2009-10 and 44 per cent
thereafter against the stipulated provision of 35 and 40 per cent respectively.

. Individual Base Schemes was restricted to 20 per cent of the total constituency
wise outlay by way of Grants-in-Aid and SGSY schemes of Rural Development
Department. However, it was scen that Department expended only 18 per cent on this
component against the provision of 20 per cent.

. Augmentation and Employment Promotion Project category was to cover the
Augmentation of projects relating to income and self-employment generation or the
programme already initiated by the Developmental department which could not be
completed due to paucity of funds. The 20 per cent of the total constituency wise
outlay year-marked for assisting groups of un-employed professionals upto 2009-10
was enhanced to 25 per cent since 2010-11 onwards. It was noticed that Department
expended 17 per cent of total constituency allocation upto 2009-10 and to 21 per cent
thereafter against the stipulated provision of 20 and 25 per cent respectively.

From the above, it is evident that the sectoral distribution of funds were not observed
in accordance with the prescribed norms to implement the programme to fill the
developmental gaps in the State except under common pool scheme. The Department
focused more attention to community projects than individual based schemes and
augmentation & employment promotion project.

77


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

The DPO, Dimapur and Mon accepted (September 2013) the facts and DPO, Kohima
and Tuensang were silent on the audit findings.

5.10.7 Supply oriented programmes

Supply oriented schemes were banned as per clause 9 of the guidelines of LADP.
Further, material required for construction/building project should be procured
directly by the DPDB in order to avoid third party involvement in the scheme.

Scrutiny of the records of four test checked DPOs revealed that Department carried
out supply oriented programmes*™ through the beneficiaries by awarding work orders
though it was banned. Further, the materials required for the construction/building
projects were also assigned to the beneficiaries in their work orders instead of
procuring directly by the DPDB.

Four test checked DPDBs issued 23 work orders to beneficiaries for procurement of
2605.25 units of CGI sheets (¥ 2.63 crore) and 235 MT rice (® 0.57 crore) as detailed
in Appendix 2.5.4.

Thus, an expenditure of हैं 3.20 crore was incurred on supply oriented schemes even
though clause 9 of the LADP guidelines specifically banned incurring expenditure on
supply oriented schemes.

The DPO, Dimapur and Tuensang accepted (September 2013) the audit finding and
stated that supply oriented schemes were encouraged due to necessity, poor economic
condition and bad monsoon in the districts and the same emerged from the grass root
to meet the exigency. The reply furnished by the DPO, Kohima was not tenable as
procurement of material were banned as per the guidelines of the programme and the
DPO, Mon did not comment on the audit findings.

During 2011-12 and 2012-13 DPO, Dimapur, issued work order to Shri Khriesali
Angami and Shri Jatin Dimasa for purchase of 1605 units of CGI sheets for हैं 0.64
crore” for distribution of CGI sheets @ 10 units to 160 households in 11 villages in 3
Dimapur III constituency and the Verification Committee also certified the utilisation
of the CGI sheets in 11 villages. However, the Village council of seven villages” out
of 11 villages authenticated the receipt of eight units of CGI sheets against the 860
units pertaining to them. Thus, it is evident that 852 units of CGI sheets valued at
%0.34 crore was misappropriated out of LADP funds by obtaining fraudulent
verification certificate from the Committee assigned for it.

The DPO, Dimapur while accepting (September 2013) the audit finding stated that the
lapses were due to unavoidable situations.

Release of payment against work executed should be measured and certified by the
technical expert group by recording the accurate measurements in the Measurement
Book in order to prepare the bill in the Running Account or by other means by the
DPOs. Though the expert technical persons drawn from CAWD/Blocks/ DRDA were

recording measurement in the measurement books. The DPOs released the payments
as per the recommendations furnished by the technical group instead of releasing the
payments through appropriate bills to ascertain the item wise work executed and

2.5.10.10 Physical and financial progr: of the programme

Quarterly progress report of the projects on physical and financial progress was
required to be submitted to the Planning and Coordination Department to facilitate
effective monitoring on the progress of implementation of the scheme and for
information to the members of SPB.

Scrutiny (June-July, 2013) of records of four test checked DPOs revealed that
Quarterly progress reports were not prepared in four test-checked DPOs. Instead
progress report on the utilisation of first installment (50 per cent of the funds) was
only forwarded to Planning Department for the release of second installment. Thus, in
the absence of quarterly progress reports, progresses in scheme implementation could
not be discussed in monthly meetings of DPDB and monitoring mechanism by the
members of SPB also became ineffective.

While accepting the audit finding, the DPO, Dimapur stated (September 2013) that
the lapses would be streamlined and three remaining DPOs were silent on the audit
finding.

11 Whether the monitoring mechanism with the Department was effective

2.5.11.1 Monitoring and evaluation

Department designed identification, execution and verification of LADP projects
through the calendar of action plan every year. DPO, the nodal officer was to
coordinate and oversee the work in consultation with technical experts at the various
levels of scheme implementation.

As per the notification (July 2004) issued by the Planning and Coordination
Department, DPDB constituted at districts should review progress of the schemes of
all developmental programmes in the district for effective implementation once a
month in the district Planning Board meetings.

However, scrutiny revealed that verification of works under LADP were assigned to
the Verification Committee in two stages according to the release of funds. The duties
of the Committee assigned for verification was limited to certify according to the
status of work in order to recommend release of payment to the beneficiaries.

Scrutiny of the minutes of monthly meetings of the DPDB revealed that the progress
of developmental programmes under LADP was not reviewed in the meetings for
effective implementation.

Department restricted the monitoring mechanism with the verification committee by
assigning the duties for verification and recommendations in order to avail subsequent
releases of funds. Thus, the nodal officer of the scheme did not oversee the work in
coordination with expert group by way of conducting inspection of the projects.

As a result of ineffective coordination in monitoring and evaluation of projects at the
level of nodal officer and deficient verification done by the verification committee,
many projects were noticed as unexecuted, short executed, deviated from approved
plan and overlapping nature were observed in audit as discussed in Paragraphs
2.5.10.4, 2.5.10.5 and 2.5.10.6.

The DPO, Dimapur accepted (September 2013) the audit finding and stated that the
progress reports/completion certificates submitted by the verification committee were
taken as authenticated reports to release the 2™ installment. Remaining three DPOs
were silent on the audit findings.



Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

2.5.12 Conclusion

Village profile was not created to identify the critical areas wherein developments
were required. Decentralised policy in selection of projects was not adopted which
resulted in random selection of projects by elected members. Inadequate technical
estimates approved by DPDB resulted in short execution, diversion of projects and
overlapping of projects. Several instances of un-executed projects were noticed and
execution of non-permissible projects outside the purview of scheme guidelines had
defeated the purpose for which the scheme was designed. Verification committee
certified works without recording measurement to support the nature of assets created.
During the period 2008-13 an amount of ¥ 270.42 crore was spent on four categorised
LADP programmes in the State. Test-checked revealed 146 non-existent projects
worth % 5.18 crore indicating possible misappropriation to that extent. Neither nodal
officer nor the technical expert group at districts monitored the projects during the
execution to assess the quality of LADP funded works.

2 Recommendatio:

> Village profile needs to be framed to identify critical areas to bridge
developmental gaps to eradicate poverty in the villages.

> Identification and selection of projects should be streamlined in order to
ensure inputs from the grass root level as envisaged in the guidelines.

> Guidelines need to be devised to structure planning process, selection criteria
of beneficiaries and preparation of technical estimates at appropriate levels to
achieve fruitful results.

> Quantity and quality control mechanism of the projects needs to be
strengthened.

> Monitoring and evaluation mechanism needs more attention and impact
analysis of the scheme should be undertaken.

PUBLIC WORKS (ROADS & BRIDGES) DEPARTMENT

Non-Lapsable ‘tral Pool of|

‘Resources (NLCPR) and North Eastern Council (NEC) in Nagaland

The Performance Audit of Roads & Bridges funded by Non-Lapsable Central Pool of
Resources (NLCPR) and the North Eastern Council (NEC) in Nagaland was
attempted to review the systems adopted by the departments and the efforts made by
the State Government to ascertain whether objectives of the scheme were met in an
economical, efficient and effective manner.

Inadequate transport facility due to lack of Roads and Bridges was a serious drawback
that crippled the development of the region. The NEC commissioned the National
Council of Applied Economic Research (NCAER) to carry out a Regional Transport
Survey to formulate a better transport system especially for inter-State connectivity
for the region based on the recommendations of a Committee of Secretaries of the

88


Chapter-II Economic Sector

Government of India (GOI). The major findings of the Performance Audit are detailed
below:

Highlights

> Concept papers forwarded to the Ministry did not contain focus areas for
developmental and economic activities to develop road infrastructure in the
State.

charge establishment by violating the order of Ministry resulting in short
execution of items of work.

(Paragraphs — 2.6.10.2 and 2.6.10.3)

> The Department released % 6.42 crore against un-executed items of work by
recording fraudulent measurements in the Measurement Books. The
Department also provided undue benefit of हैं 2.81 crore to contractors by

approved cost.

(Paragraphs — 2.6.11.5.2; 2.6.11.5.8; 2.6.11.5.9 and 2.6.11.5.10)

2

GOI created a Non Lapsable Central Pool of Resources (NLCPR) in the Union
Budget for the year 1998-99 in the public account titled “Central Resource Pool for
Development of NER” for funding specific programmes for economic and social
upliftment of North Eastern States to ensure speedy development of Roads and
Bridges in NER and also to develop the physical and social infrastructure for
providing support under the Central Pool.

1 Introduction

The North East has essentially depended on Central funding for development works.
All the States in the NER are Special Category States whose Development Plans are
centrally financed on the basis of 90 per cent grant and 10 per cent loan.

89


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

The North Eastern Council (NEC), constituted in 1971 by an Act of the Parliament
is the nodal agency for the economic and social development of the North Eastern
Region which consists of the eight states and has marked the beginning of a new
chapter of concerted and planned efforts for the rapid development of the Region.
Over the last thirty five years, NEC has been instrumental in setting in motion a new
economic endeavour aimed at providing the basic facilities.

NLCPR/NEC Scheme headed by a Chairman. The organisational structure for
implementation of NLCPR/NEC funded projects on Roads and Bridges in Nagaland
is structured below:

2.6.3 Scope of Audit

The Performance Audit covered the period from 2008-2013 through test check of
records of the Chief Engineer, Public Works (Roads & Bridges), 14 Divisions out of
24 headed by Executive Engineers,16 projects out of 42°* NLCPR projects and 4 out
of 8 NEC funded projects in the State during April 2013 to August 2013. Further,
audit also checked the remedial action taken by the State Government on the audit
observations made by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India in the Union
Report (2007-08) and State Report (2010-11).

The broad objectives of the Performance Audit on roads and bridges were to assess
whether:

» There was a critical assessment of needs in each of the infrastructural areas
and that the individual projects were planned appropriately.

2.6.6 Audit Methodology

The Performance Audit commenced with an entry conference with the officers of the
Public Works (Roads and Bridges) Department to discuss the objectives, scope,
methodology etc. on 17 April 2013. A total of 20 Projects (16 out of 42 NLCPR and 4
out of 8 NEC) were selected on Probability Proportional to Size Without Replacement
(PPSWOR) method.

Scrutiny of records in audit was done at the Office of the Chief Engineer, Public
Works Department (Roads & Bridges), Nagaland and 14 Divisions out of 24
Divisions headed by Executive Engineers (R&B). All the 20 selected projects
covering 14 Divisions were physically verified by audit along with the Departmental
officers. The Audit findings were discussed in an exit conference held on 3 October
2013. The replies of the Department have duly been incorporated in the Report at
appropriate places.

The office of the Accountant General (Audit), Nagaland places on record our sincere
appreciation for the co-operation of the Chief Engineer, PWD (R&B) and designated
Officers under Public Works Department in the various divisions.

AUDIT FINDINGS
Audit Objective - 1

| areas and

1 Preparation of concept note

NLCPR guidelines provides that the Annual priority list of projects should be
supported with “Concept papers” for all the projects elaborating the problems to be
addressed through the project at local and State level detailing the nature and
magnitude of the problems, the development objective proposed to be achieved,
benefits likely to accrue in terms of population and other parameters. It should also
contain the ongoing initiative taken by the State Government to avoid duplication and
synergy created through the proposed projects, economic parameters to justify the
funding and social infrastructure and socio-economic analysis, estimated cost,
financial and physical phasing, timeframe etc. for implementation of the projects.
Issues relating to sustainability including operation and maintenance of assets after
project completion and subsequent issues should also be elaborated while formulating
the concept paper. In addition, the Union Ministry had circulated a District
Infrastructure Index (DII) in September 2009 in order to help in better targeting of
schemes and projects within NER. This was to be followed by all the States under
NER.

Department did not furnish to audit the list of projects proposed along with the
concept note pertaining to 2008-09 though the Ministry sanctioned 12 projects during
the year. Out of 139 projects included in the priority list pertaining to 2009-10 to
2012-13, Ministry sanctioned 20 projects (14 per cent). Scrutiny of list of projects
forwarded and approved by the Ministry during 2009-10 to 2012-13 revealed that
three projects were sanctioned outside the priority list. Details are given below:

Table No. 2.6.1

2009-10 1 06.08 2009
2011-12 1 13.05.2011 लि

(Source: Departmental figures)

Scrutiny (June 2013) revealed that 90 concept papers only were available against the
priority list of 139 projects prepared and forwarded to Ministry resulting in failure in
gap analysis for preliminary survey in respect of 49 projects. The concept paper only
focused on estimated cost and briefed the requirement of transportation as per the
remoteness of the village in order to improve the living standards of the people

92


Chapter-II Economic Sector

surrounding that area. Thus, the important areas wherein more developmental and
economic activities to accrue benefits in terms of socio-infrastructure developments
required to be focused as per the guidelines were not available in the concept paper
forwarded to the Ministry. Besides, the District Infrastructure Index (DII) method was
also not adopted to correctly identify the Roads and Bridges projects required for the
State.

The Department accepted (October 2013) the audit findings and stated that the
contents of the Concept Papers would be improved by incorporating all the required
aspects justifying the need for the road works.

on of priority

As per the time frame fixed by the Ministry, each State would propose through its
Nodal Department, an annual profile of projects under the NLCPR in terms of priority
list latest by 31 December every year for the next financial year.

However, scrutiny (June 2013) revealed that during the period from 2009-13, the
Department submitted the list of projects from May to August of the subsequent year
resulting in a delay ranging from five to eight months which consequently affected the
implementation of the projects.

Department accepted (October 2013) the audit findings and stated that the submission
of priority list was delayed due to the time consumed at the State Government level to
finalise the list of projects.

2.6.9 The mechanism in place for approval of the projects was strictly adhered

to and appropriate checks applied at each stage prior to approval and

2.6.9.1 Project formulation through Detailed Project Report

A detailed project report (DPR) should be prepared for each project proposal. The
DPR should be accompanied by a socio-economic feasibility report and must
establish technical and economic viability such as its rationale, cost, finances
available from other sources and detailed technical specification, etc. In addition to
this, the DPR should clearly laydown Critical Path Method (CPM) and Programme
Evaluation and Review Technique (PERT) chart, year-wise phasing of input, project
monitoring indicators, quarterly and year-wise physical output to be achieved, project
implementation schedule and all regulatory and statutory clearances etc.

Scrutiny (June 2013) revealed that the DPRs pertaining to all the 20 selected projects
were available in two volumes viz. Technical Report and Costing Report. In the
Technical Report technical viability of the project was discussed in addition to the
socio-economic benefits and in the costing report, cost analysis of the project was
done. However, CPM and PERT chart, year-wise phasing of input, project monitoring
indicators, quarterly and year-wise physical output to be achieved, project

93


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

implementation schedule and all regulatory and statutory clearances etc. were not
incorporated as per the model DPR incorporated in the guidelines of the Ministry.

Thus, in the absence of above adequate data in the DPR, there was irregular release of
funds, poor project monitoring mechanism, time over run and deviations from the
objectives framed for the projects were also noticed as discussed in paragraph No.
2.6.1.5.

The Ministry communicated (January 2011) the deficiencies noticed in the DPRs to
the Chief Engineer, PWD (R&B) to ensure that the DPRs were free from common
mistakes and deficiencies enabling the competent authority to pass the proposals
quickly.

The Department accepted (October 2013) the deficiencies in DPRs pointed out by
audit and assured that care would be taken henceforth to frame the DPRs according to
the norms prescribed by the Ministry.

2.6.9.2 Cost of preparation of DPRs

While awarding the Administrative approval of the projects, the Ministry directed the
State Government to pay the remuneration for vetting the DPRs of the projects either
to the State Technical Agency (STA), Jorhat Engineering College, Jorhat or to the
empanelled consultants of the Ministry. It further restricted that the provision of 3 per
cent contingencies was not to be utilised for any charges towards consultancy.
Scrutiny (May to July 2013) of the records revealed that the DPRs prepared during
2008-09 to 2012-13 (29 NLCPR projects) were not vetted by the State Technical
Agency, Jorhat Engineering College, Jorhat. Instead, all the formalities were
completed at the level of the Chief Engineer, PWD, R&B, Nagaland.

It was further noticed that the Department also released रें 1.64 crore to four agencies
out of the contingencies provisioned in the scheme towards the cost of consultancy
charges for preparation of DPRs as detailed in the following table:

2.6.9.3_Technical estimates

As per the manual for survey, investigation and preparation of road projects published
by the Indian Road Congress (IRC), pre-feasibility study, preliminary project report
preparation feasibility study and detailed engineering and plan of construction should
be prepared on the basis of reconnaissance survey by collecting information on the
present status of the road, deficiency/distress identification, development potential,
environmental impact, traffic data (present and future) and approximate estimation of
cost. The economic analysis may involve traffic allocation studies, assessment of
resource generation potential, funding pattern and risk. Location of toll plaza may also
need to be identified. The feasibility study was intended to establish soundness of
engineering design. The Detailed engineering would include topographical survey,

location survey, soil survey, drainage study and environment management action
plan. The comprehensive study would be the background of the DPR design report
required for technical and financial sanction.

Scrutiny (May to July 2013) of the technical estimate incorporated in the DPRs of
selected projects revealed that the points viz. Soil survey, Drainage study,
Environment management action plan and Future traffic data analysis were not
analysed as per the standards prescribed by the IRC.

95


Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

In the absence of the above data, the Divisional Executive Engineers revised the items
in the DPRs as well as estimates approved by the Ministry. This further resulted in
cost escalation of items approved by the Ministry and time over run of the projects.

The aforementioned deficiencies in the technical estimates were highlighted
(December 2010) by the Ministry of Roads and Transport.

While accepting (October 2013) the audit findings, the Department stated that due to
shortage of time awarded for preparation of DPRs all the technical procedures could
not be followed.

While approving the projects, the Ministry laid down the condition that the funds shall
be utilised within specified time and strictly for which they were sanctioned and no
diversion of funds was allowed.

GOI approved (June 2011) a project namely “Improvement and up-gradation of road
from Border road (19 Km) to Changlangshu in Mon district” under NLCPR at an
estimated cost of € 6.11 crore and work was awarded for हैं 5.99 crore as per the rates
quoted (SOR 2008).

Scrutiny (January 2013) of the records of Chief Engineer, PWD (R&B), Kohima
revealed that the Department revised (November 2011) the items and rates of the bill
of quantity through a corrigendum and stipulated the total cost of the project at = 5.99
crore based on the Schedule of Rates (SOR 2010) by excluding one item of work
(construction of protection wall works for = 1.21 crore) approved by the Ministry.
Thus, the cost escalation of revised items was met by compromising one major item
of work approved by the GOI.

While replying to audit findings the Department stated (October 2013) that the DPR
was framed based on the rates of SOR 2008 whereas the approval was conveyed in
2011 only and by that time SOR 2008 had been updated to SOR 2010. Therefore, in
order to carry out the work within the sanctioned amount, some items were deleted as
per the site conditions. The reply was not tenable as Ministry did not communicate
approval for the deviation.

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

Audit Objective - 5

2.6.12 Impact of the scheme was analyzed at various levels

Out of the 32 NLCPR projects” taken up by the Public Works Department (29
projects), Department of Tourism (1 project) and Department of Geology and Mining
(2 projects) during 2008-09 to 2012-13, 9 projects (eight roads and one bridge) were
completed as of March 2013 leaving 23 ongoing projects (21 roads and two bridges).

As per the physical progress report, roads of 404.44 Km” (74 per cent) length were
completed against the approved stretch of 535 Km.

2.6.12.1 Status of all-weather standard roads

The broad objective of the NLCPR scheme was to ensure speedy development of
roads and bridges infrastructure in North Eastern Region with a long term vision to
improve economic well being of the people in the region. Accordingly, the GOI
allocated funds for the projects identified by the State to create all weather standard
roads.

The Department of Public Works stated that by utilising NLCPR funds of € 1022.56
crore since inception of the scheme, roads of 1196.7 Km length were constructed in
the State out of which 1141.34 Km (95 per cent) length of roads got blacktopped and
roads of 55.36 Km (5 per cent) length remained without blacktop as of June 2013.

Audit conducted joint physical verification of 18 projects wherein 26 roads in
11 divisions” were covered for a total length of 794.54 Kms. Out of 794.54 Km of
roads, as per the administrative and technical sanction, 765.54 Kms stretch was
required to be blacktopped. Joint physical verification (April to August 2013)
revealed that only 277.67 Km (36 per cent) stretch was blacktopped to meet the all-
weather standard resulting in 64 per cent of un-surfaced road in the 11 test checked
divisions as detailed in Appendix 2.6.3. This indicated that the data provided by the
Department of Public Works did not match with the ground reality.

Development of transportation sector in accordance with socio

economic factor- Maintenance of the assets created

Post implementation management issues relating to sustainability including
stakeholder commitment, operation and maintenance of assets after project
completion and other related issues should be addressed in “sustainability” section of
DPR.

Chapter-II Economic Sector

The State Government while submitting the concept paper along with the priority list
to the Ministry should discuss the issues relating to sustainability including operation
and maintenance of assets after project completion.

Scrutiny of the concept papers revealed that the issues relating to sustainability
including operation and maintenance of assets after project completion were not
discussed. Further, no specific funds were allocated for the maintenance of assets
created which resulted in the poor status of the roads and bridges created out of the
NLCPR funds. The Department of Public Works did not carry out any survey to
identify the benefits extended to the public as envisaged in the approved DPR. Hence,
the quantum of benefit availed through the assets created under NLCPR funds could
not be ascertained in audit.

2.6.12.3 Improvement/upgradation of existing roads and bridges constructed

under NLCPR/NEC pro.

While formulating the concept papers for priority projects, the Department had to
incorporate the issues relating to sustainability including operation and maintenance
of assets after project completion and related issues. However, the Department neither
incorporated such issues nor prepared any DPR for maintenance of roads constructed
under NLCPR/NEC projects.

Scrutiny revealed that the Department sanctioned (March 2012) ₹ 2.89 crore under
State Plan for only one road viz. “Maintenance of road from Medziphema to
Athibung” constructed under NEC. The following observations were noticed in the
execution of the mainternance work:

Audit Report for the year ended 31 March 2013

Audit Objective - 7

for adequate and effective moni

rterly Review meetings

The project wise progress of implementation should be reported on a quarterly basis.
The Chief Secretary should hold quarterly meetings to review the progress of
implementation of the ongoing projects under NLCPR and summary of such meetings
should be made available to the Ministry of DoNER. State would carry out project
inspection periodically and the quarterly review report should contain distinct section
on the finding of the project inspection. State would also nominate a nodal officer for
each project who would be responsible for project implementation and monitoring.
The monitoring and evaluation mechanism would also be undertaken through impact
studies, social audit and evaluation conducted by Government or through independent
agencies.

The Department of Public Works could carry out monitoring and evaluation of only है
projects” (25 per cent) out of 32 projects funded by NEC/NLCPR. In the absence of
project inspection report, audit could not even analyse the outcome of the evaluation
of 8 projects carried out by the Department. The Department did not carry out any
survey to ascertain the efficacy and effectiveness of the operation of infrastructure
created to measure the impact on the targeted population as per the objective
projected in the DPR. Further, evaluation through independent agencies was not
carried out to analyse the results of the infrastructure created out of NLCPR/NEC
funds.

As envisaged in the guidelines of the scheme, the State Government should carry out
periodical inspection at various levels for an effective system of reporting and
monitoring of the projects. The results of the inspection reports of the projects should
be discussed in the quarterly review meetings of the Committee headed by the
Commissioner and Secretary to the GON, Public Works Department.

Though the Department of Public Works replied that the quarterly review meetings
were conducted for analysing the progress of the projects, documentary evidence
(minutes of the meetings) were not furnished to audit.

While conveying the administrative sanction, the Ministry stipulated that the ground
inspection of the projects should be carried out at least once in a quarter and the
physical progress report should be submitted within 2 weeks after the close of every
